\sup_{j}\int_{c-h}^{c+h}\frac{|\psi_{j}|^{2}}{(\Xi-\textrm{Re}\,z_{j})^{2}+(\textrm{Im}\,z_{j})^{2}}<\infty\qquad\forall h<\delta\,.

If
\psi(c)
were different from
0
, we can select a positive
h<\delta
and a positive
c_{0}
with the property that
|\psi(t)|^{2}\geq 2c_{0}
for all
t\in[c-h,c+h]
. In particular, for a large enough
j
we infer
|\psi_{j}(t)|^{2}\geq c
for all
t\in[c-\delta,c+\delta]
. But then we would conclude

\sup_{j}\int_{c-h}^{c+h}\frac{1}{(\Xi-\textrm{Re}\,z_{j})^{2}+(\textrm{Im}\,z_{j})^{2}}<\infty\,.

Since the denominator converges to
(\Xi-\Xi(c))^{2}
, this is clearly not possible.

We now wish to pass in the limit in (
4.67
) to derive that

-\psi^{\prime\prime}+m_{0}^{2}\psi+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(c)}\psi=0\,,

(4.72)

where we notice that, thanks to
\psi(c)=0
and the Hölder regularity of
\psi
, the function
\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(c)}\psi
is indeed in
L^{p}
for every
p<2
. We thus understand the equation distributionally.
The equation clearly passes to the limit outside the singularity
c
of the denominator and thus we just need to pass it to the limit distributionally in some interval
]c-h,c+h[
. We write the third term as

\displaystyle\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{d}{dt}\ln(\Xi-z_{j})\right)\frac{A}{\Xi^{\prime}}\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\ln(\Xi-z_{j})\frac{A}{\Xi^{\prime}}\psi_{j}\right)-\ln(\Xi-z_{j})\frac{A}{\Xi^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}_{j}-\ln(\Xi-z_{j})\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{A}{\Xi^{\prime}}\right)\psi_{j}\,.

Observe that we can define the logarithm unequivocally because
\Xi
is real valued and
\textrm{Im}\,z_{j}>0
.

Next, we remark that:

(i)

\frac{A}{\Xi^{\prime}}
is smooth in
]c-h,c+h[
;

(ii)

\ln(\Xi-z_{j})
converges strongly
1
1

1

Since
\ln(\Xi-z_{j})
converges uniformly to
\ln(\Xi-\Xi(c))
on any compact set which does not contain
c
, in order to reach the conclusion it suffices to prove a uniform
L^{q}
bound on the functions, for every
q<\infty
. This can be easily concluded as follows. Choose an interval
[c-h,c+h]
and recall that
\Xi
does not change sign on it. For each
j
large enough we then find a unique
c_{j}\in[c-h,c+h]
such that
\Xi(c_{j})=\textrm{Re}\,z_{j}
. Using the mean value theorem we easily conclude that
|\Xi(t)-z_{j}|\geq|\Xi(t)-\Xi(c_{j})|\geq C^{-1}|t-c_{j}|
for every
t\in[c-h,c+h]
, where
C^{-1}=\min\{|\Xi^{\prime}(t)|:c-h\leq t\leq c+h\}
.
to
\ln(\Xi-\Xi(c))
in
L^{q}(]c-h,c+h[)
for every
q<\infty
;

(iii)

\psi_{j}^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime}
weakly in
L^{2}
, while
\psi_{j}\to\psi
uniformly.

We thus conclude that
\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\psi_{j}
converges distributionally to

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\ln(\Xi-\Xi(c))\frac{A}{\Xi^{\prime}}\psi\right)-\ln(\Xi-\Xi(c))\frac{A}{\Xi^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}-\ln(\Xi-\Xi(c))\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{A}{\Xi^{\prime}}\right)\psi\,.

Using now that
\psi\in W^{1,2}
and
\psi(c)=0
we can rewrite the latter distribution as

\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(c)}\psi

and hence conclude the validity of (
4.72
).

Observe next that from (
4.72
) we infer
\psi^{\prime\prime}\in L^{p}
for every
p<2
, which in turn implies that
\psi
is indeed
C^{1,\kappa}_{\text{loc}}
for every
\kappa<\frac{1}{2}
. In turn this implies that
\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(c)}\psi
is continuous at
c
, so that in particular
\psi
is twice differentiable. We thus can argue as for the derivation of (
4.61
) and get

\int\Big(\Big(\psi^{\prime}-\frac{\Xi^{\prime}}{\Xi-\Xi(c)}\psi\Big)^{2}+m_{0}^{2}\psi^{2}\Big)+2\int\frac{\Xi^{\prime}}{\Xi-\Xi(c)}\psi^{2}=0\,.

(4.73)

Once again we can set
\psi=(\Xi-\Xi(c))\eta
and observe that
\eta\in W^{1,2}
, to rewrite the latter identity as

\int((\Xi-\Xi(c))^{2}(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)^{2}+(m_{0}^{2}-1)(\Xi-\Xi(c))^{2}\eta^{2})=0\,,

inferring that
\eta=0
.

We thus have concluded that
\psi
vanishes identically, but this is not yet a contradiction since the normalization (
4.68
) and the strong
L^{2}
convergence does not ensure that
\psi
is nontrivial. In order to complete our argument, note first that, by the monotonicity of
\Xi
, for each
j
large enough there is a unique
c_{j}
such that
\Xi(c_{j})=\textrm{Re}\,z_{j}
. We then multiply the equation (
4.67
) by
\bar{\psi}_{j}-\overline{\psi_{j}(c_{j})}
to obtain

\int\Big(|\psi_{j}^{\prime}|^{2}+m_{j}^{2}\psi_{j}(\bar{\psi}_{j}-\overline{\psi_{j}(c_{j})})+\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\psi_{j}(\bar{\psi}_{j}-\overline{\psi_{j}(c_{j})})\Big)=0\,.

Note that
c_{j}
must converge to
c
and that the integrals

\int\psi_{j}(\bar{\psi}_{j}-\overline{\psi_{j}(c_{j})})

converges to
0
because
\psi_{j}-\psi_{j}(c_{j})
converges to
0
uniformly and, thanks to the uniform exponential decay of
\psi_{j}
, the latter are uniformly bounded in
L^{1}
. For the same reason the first integral in the sum

\int_{|t-c|\geq h}\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\psi_{j}(\bar{\psi}_{j}-\overline{\psi_{j}(c_{j})})+\int_{|t-c|\leq h}\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\psi_{j}(\bar{\psi}_{j}-\overline{\psi_{j}(c_{j})})

(4.74)

converges to
0
for every fixed
h
. On the other hand,
|\frac{A(t)}{\Xi(t)-z_{j}}||\psi_{j}(t)-\psi_{j}(c_{j})|\leq C|t-c_{j}|^{-1/2}
and thus the second integrand in (
4.74
)
converges to
0
as well. We thus conclude that the
L^{2}
norm of
\psi^{\prime}_{j}
converges to
0
as well. This however contradicts the normalization (
4.68
).

4.7.
Proof of Proposition
4.4.3
: Part I

We set
m_{0}=m_{a}
,
z_{0}=\Xi(a)
, and
we fix a
\psi_{0}
solution of

-\frac{d^{2}\psi_{0}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\psi_{0}+\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi_{0}=0

with
L^{2}
norm equal
1
. Since the operator is self-adjoint we will indeed assume that
\psi_{0}
is real. We then define the projector
P_{0}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})\to\{\kappa\psi_{0}:\kappa\in\mathbb{C}\}
as

P_{0}(\psi)=\langle\psi,\psi_{0}\rangle\psi_{0}\,.

Observe that
P_{0}
is self-adjoint.
Next,
in a neighborhood of
(m_{0},z_{0})
we will look for solutions of (
4.17
) by solving

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\psi^{\prime\prime}+m^{2}\psi+\frac{A}{\Xi-z}\psi+P_{0}(\psi)=\psi_{0}\\
\\
\langle\psi,\psi_{0}\rangle=1\end{array}\right.

(4.75)

which we can rewrite as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\psi^{\prime\prime}+m_{0}^{2}\psi+\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi+P_{0}(\psi)=A\left(((\Xi-z_{0})^{-1}-(\Xi-z)^{-1})\psi\right)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(m_{0}^{2}-m^{2})\psi+\psi_{0}\\
\\
\langle\psi,\psi_{0}\rangle=1\end{array}\right.

(4.76)

Next we observe that the operator
-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}
, considered as a closed unbounded self-adjoint operator in
L^{2}
(with domain
W^{2,2}
) has an inverse
\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\colon L^{2}\to L^{2}
which is a bounded operator. We thus rewrite (
4.76
) as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\underbrace{\psi+\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\left(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi+P_{0}(\psi)\right)}_{=:T(\psi)}\\
\qquad\qquad=\underbrace{\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\left(\left(A\left((\Xi-z_{0})^{-1}-(\Xi-z)^{-1}\right)+(m_{0}^{2}-m^{2})\right)\psi\right)}_{=:-\mathcal{R}_{m,z}(\psi)}+\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}(\psi_{0})\\
\\
\langle\psi,\psi_{0}\rangle=1\,.\end{array}\right.

(4.77)

The proof of Proposition
4.4.3
will then be broken into two pieces. In this section we will show the first part, which we can summarize in the following

Lemma 4.7.1
.

For every
\mu>0
, if
(m,z)
sufficiently close to
(m_{0},z_{0})
and
\textrm{Im}\,z\geq\mu|\textrm{Re}\,(z-z_{0})|
then there is a unique
\psi=\psi(m,z)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
solving

T(\psi)+\mathcal{R}_{m,z}(\psi)=\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}(\psi_{0})\,.

(4.78)

We recognize this as, essentially, the first step in the Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction but written for the solution
\psi
rather than the perturbation
\tilde{\psi}=\psi-\psi_{0}
. (The second step will be to the solve one-dimensional equation (
4.86
) enforcing
\langle\psi,\psi_{0}\rangle=1
.)

Before coming to the proof of Lemma
4.7.1
, we single out two important ingredients.

Lemma 4.7.2
.

T
is a bounded operator with bounded inverse on the spaces
L^{2}
and
C^{\sigma}
, for any
\sigma\in]0,1[
.

Proof.

Recall that the operator
\mathcal{K}_{m}
is given by the convolution with
\frac{1}{2m}e^{-m|\cdot|}
.
Observe that
\mathcal{K}_{m}
is well-defined on
C^{\sigma}
and so is the multiplication by
\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}
, since the latter is a smooth functions with bounded derivatives, and the operator
P_{0}(\psi)=\langle\psi,\psi_{0}\rangle\psi_{0}
: for the latter we just need to check that
\psi\overline{\psi_{0}}
is integrable, which follows from the exponential decay of
\psi_{0}
, cf. Corollary
4.5.3
.

In this first step we prove that
T
is a bounded operator with bounded inverse in the spaces
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
C^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R})
.

Recall that
\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}=\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}
is indeed a bounded smooth function (thanks to the structural assumptions on
\Xi
: in particular recall that
\Xi^{\prime}(a)\neq 0
and
A(a)=0
, which implies that
\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}
is in fact smooth at
a
). Moreover the function and its derivatives decay exponentially at
\pm\infty
. It follows therefore that
\psi\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi+P_{0}(\psi))
is a compact operator, both on
L^{2}
and on
C^{\sigma}
. Thus
T
is a Fredholm operator with index
0
. We thus just need to check that the kernel is
0
in order to conclude that it is invertible with bounded inverse. In both cases we need to show that the equation

-\frac{d^{2}\psi}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\psi+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}\psi+P_{0}(\psi)=0

(4.79)

has only the trivial solution. Observe that the kernel
V
of the operator
\psi\mapsto-\frac{d^{2}\psi}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\psi+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}\psi
is
1
-dimensional by Lemma
4.5.2
and Corollary
4.5.3
. In particular
V
is generated by
\psi_{0}
. Since the operator
P_{0}
is the orthogonal projection onto
V
and
-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}
is self-adjoint, the kernel of
-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}+P_{0}
in
L^{2}
must be trivial.

In order to argue that the kernel is
0
on
C^{\sigma}
we apply a variation of the same idea: first we observe that if
\psi
is a
C^{\sigma}
solution of (
4.79
), then
\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}\psi+P_{0}(\psi)
is also in
C^{\sigma}
and hence
\psi^{\prime\prime}\in C^{\sigma}
. Observe also that the operator is self-adjoint and thus we can assume that
\psi
is real-valued. We then multiply both sides of (
4.79
) by
\bar{\psi}_{0}
, integrate by parts and use the fact that
\psi_{0}
is in the kernel of the self-adjoint operator
-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}
to conclude that
(\langle\psi,\psi_{0}\rangle)^{2}=0
. But then

\psi
is a bounded solution of
-\frac{d\psi}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\psi+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}\psi=0
. Given that
\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}\psi
is a product of an exponentially decaying function and a bounded function, we conclude that
-\frac{d^{2}\psi}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\psi
is an exponentially decaying function
f
. We thus have
\psi=\mathcal{K}_{m}(f)+C_{1}e^{-m_{0}t}+C_{2}e^{m_{0}t}
for two constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
. However
\mathcal{K}_{m}(f)
decays exponentially at both
\pm\infty
and thus, given that
\psi
is bounded, we must have
C_{1}=C_{2}=0
. In particular
\psi
decays exponentially at both
\pm\infty
and so it is an
L^{2}
function. But we already saw that every
L^{2}
solution is trivial.
∎

Lemma 4.7.3
.

For every constant
\mu>0
we define the cone
C_{\mu}:=\{z:\textrm{Im}z\geq\mu|\textrm{Re}\,(z-z_{0})|\}
. Then

\lim_{z\in C_{\mu},(m,z)\to(m_{0},z_{0})}\|\mathcal{R}_{m,z}\|_{O}=0\,,

(4.80)

where
\|L\|_{O}
is the operator norm of
L

when considered as a bounded operator from
L^{2}
to
L^{2}
.

Proof.

Clearly, it suffices to show that

\lim_{z\in C_{\mu},z\to z_{0}}\|\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\circ(A/(\Xi-z)-A/(\Xi-z_{0}))\|_{O}=0\,.

(4.81)

We can rewrite the operator as

\psi\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\Big(\frac{A(z-z_{0})}{(\Xi-z)(\Xi-z_{0})}\psi\Big)\,.

First of all observe that the operators

\psi\mapsto L_{z}(\psi)=\frac{A(z-z_{0})}{(\Xi-z)(\Xi-z_{0})}\psi

are bounded in the operator norm uniformly in
z\in C_{\mu}
by a constant
M
. Moreover, we can see that the adjoint operator is given by
L_{z}^{*}(\psi)=\frac{A(\bar{z}-z_{0})}{(\Xi-\bar{z})(\Xi-z_{0})}\psi
converges strongly in
L^{2}
to
0
: indeed the functions
\frac{A(\bar{z}-z_{0})}{(\Xi-\bar{z})(\Xi-z_{0})}
are uniformly bounded and they converge to
0
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{a\}
. We now use an argument entirely similar to that used in the proof of Lemma
3.3.2
: given any
\varepsilon>0
we fix the orthogonal projection
P_{N}
onto a finite-dimensional subspace
V
of
L^{2}
with the property that
\|\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\circ P_{N}-\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\|_{O}
is smaller than
\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}
. We then argue that for
|z-z_{0}|
sufficiently small
P_{N}\circ L_{z}
has operator norm smaller than
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Having chosen an orthonormal base
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{N}
for
V
, we recall that

P_{N}(\psi)=\sum_{i}\langle\psi_{i},\psi\rangle\psi_{i}\,

(where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the scalar product in
L^{2}
).
Therefore our claim amounts to show that

|\langle\psi_{i},L_{z}(\psi)\rangle|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2N}

for
z
sufficiently close to
z_{0}
and every
\psi
with
\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}\leq 1
. For the latter we use

|\langle\psi_{i},L_{z}(\psi)\rangle|=|\langle L_{z}^{*}(\psi_{i}),\psi\rangle|\leq\|L_{z}^{*}(\psi_{i})\|_{L^{2}}\,.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.7.1
.

We rewrite the equation that we want to solve as

\psi+T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m,z}(\psi)=T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}(\psi_{0})\,.

Note that
P_{0}(\psi_{0})=\psi_{0}
. Furthermore, since
\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}
is, by definition, the inverse operator of
-\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\operatorname{Id}
,

\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}^{-1}\Big(\psi_{0}+\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\Big(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi_{0}\Big)\Big)=-\psi_{0}^{\prime\prime}+m_{0}^{2}\psi_{0}+\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi_{0}=0.

Therefore,

\psi_{0}+\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\Big(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi_{0}\Big)=0.

In combination with the definition of
T
in (
4.77
), we get

T(\psi_{0})=\psi_{0}+\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\Big(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi_{0}+\psi_{0}\Big)=\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}(\psi_{0}),

in other words,

T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}(\psi_{0})=\psi_{0}\,.

(4.82)

Therefore, (
4.78
) becomes

(\operatorname{Id}+T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m,z})(\psi)=\psi_{0}\,,

(4.83)

so the existence of a unique solution is guaranteed as soon as
\|T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m,z}\|_{O}<1
.
∎

Remark 4.7.4
.

In the remaining part of the proof of Proposition
4.4.3
we will take advantage of the representation of
\psi
as a function of
\psi_{0}
through the Neumann series coming from (
4.83
). More precisely, our proof of Lemma
4.7.1
leads to the following representation:

\psi=\psi_{0}-(T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m,z})(\psi_{0})+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}(T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m,z})^{k}(\psi_{0})\,.

(4.84)

4.8.
Proof of Proposition
4.4.3
: Part II

We now complete the proof of Proposition
4.4.3
. The positive parameter
\mu>0
in Lemma
4.7.1
will have to be chosen sufficiently small: its choice will be specified in a few paragraphs, while for the moment we assume it to be fixed. We set
m_{0}=m_{a}
and
z_{0}=\Xi(a)
. Thus, for each
(m_{0}+h,z)
in a set

U_{\delta,\mu}:=\{|h|<\delta,|z-z_{0}|<\delta,\textrm{Im}z>\mu|\textrm{Re}\,(z-z_{0})|\}

we know that that there is a solution
\psi=\psi(m_{0}+h,z)
of (
4.78
) which moreover satisfies the expansion (
4.84
).
We then define the function

H(h,z):=\langle\psi(m_{0}+h,z),\psi_{0}\rangle\,,

(4.85)

and obviously we are looking for those
z
which solve

H(h,z)=1\,.

(4.86)

The main point of our analysis is the following

Lemma 4.8.1
.

The function
H
is holomorphic in
z
and moreover

H(h,z)=1-2m_{a}h+c(a)(z-z_{0})+o(|z-z_{0}|+|h|)

(4.87)

where
c(a)
is a complex number with
\textrm{Im}\,c(a)>0
.

The coefficient
c(a)
is computed in (
4.8
) and is, in fact,
1/z_{1}
from (
4.35
) with
\textrm{Im}z_{1}<0
and
m_{1}=-(2m_{0})^{-1}
. The formal computation involving the Plemelj formula to obtain (
4.35
) will appear in the calculation of
c(a)
below.

Given Lemma
4.8.1
, consider now
\xi(h)
which we obtain by solving
c(a)(\xi-z_{0})=2m_{a}h
, namely,

\xi(h)=\frac{2m_{a}h}{c(a)}+z_{0}=\frac{2m_{a}h}{|c(a)|^{2}}\overline{c(a)}+z_{0}\,.

The idea behind the latter definition is that, if the term
o(|z-z_{0}|+|h|)
vanished identically,
z=\xi(h)
would be the solution of
H(h,z)=1
. Even though
o(|z-z_{0}|+|h|)
does not vanish, we nonetheless expect that the solution
z
of
H(h,z)=1
is relatively close to
\xi(h)
.

Since
\textrm{Im}\,c(a)>0
,
\xi(h)
has positive imaginary part if
h<0
. In particular we have

\textrm{Im}\,\xi(h)\geq\gamma|h|\qquad\forall h<0\,.

where
\gamma
is a positive constant. We then rewrite

H(h,z)=1+c(a)(z-\xi(h))+\underbrace{o(|\xi(h)-z_{0}|+h)}_{=:r(h)}+o(|z-\xi(h)|)\,.

Consider the disk
D_{h}:=\{|z-\xi(h)|\leq 2\beta|h|\}
, for a suitably chosen constant
\beta>0
. We will show below that adjusting the constants
\mu
and
\beta
suitably, the disk will be in the domain of the holomorphic function
H(\cdot,z)
. Leaving this aside for the moment, by Rouché Theorem, if we choose
h
sufficiently small the set
H(h,D_{h})
contains a disk of radius
|c(a)|\beta h
centered at
1+r(h)
. But then for
h
sufficiently small we also have
|r(h)|\leq\frac{|c(a)|\beta h}{2}
and so we conclude that
1\in H(h,D_{h})
, namely that there is a point
z(h)
in the disk
D_{h}
which is mapped in
1
by
H(h,\cdot)
. This would then complete the proof of Proposition
4.4.3
if we were able to prove that
\textrm{Im}\,z(h)>0
. We therefore need to show that
D_{h}
is in the domain of
H(h,\cdot)
, namely,

\textrm{Im}\,z\geq\mu|\textrm{Re}\,(z-z_{0})|\,\qquad\forall z\in D_{h}\,.

We first estimate

\textrm{Im}\,z\geq\,\textrm{Im}\,\xi(h)-2\beta|h|\geq(\gamma-2\beta)|h|\,.

Then

|\textrm{Re}\,z-z_{0}|\leq|\xi(h)-z_{0}|+|z-\xi(h)|\leq\left(\frac{2m_{a}}{|c(a)|}+2\beta\right)|h|\,.

(4.88)

We thus conclude that

\textrm{Im}\,z(h)\geq\frac{\gamma-2\beta}{2m_{a}|c(a)|^{-1}+2\beta}|\textrm{Re}\,(z(h)-z_{0})|\,.

(4.89)

Thus it suffices to choose
\beta=\frac{\gamma}{3}
and
\mu=\frac{\gamma}{6m_{a}|c(a)|^{-1}+2\gamma}
. This guarantees at the same time the existence of a solution and the fact that
z(h)
has positive imaginary part when
h<0
(which results from combining (
4.88
) and (
4.89
).

In order to complete the proof of Proposition
4.4.3
we therefore just need to show Lemma
4.8.1
.

Proof of Lemma
4.8.1
.

In order to show holomorphicity we just need to show that, for each fixed
z
,

z\mapsto\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m,z})^{k}

is holomorphic. Since the series converges in the operator norm, it suffices to show that each map
z\mapsto(-T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m,z})^{k}
is holomorphic for every
k
, for which indeed it suffices to show that
z\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{m,z}
is holomorphic. This is however obvious from the explicit formula. We therefore now come to the Taylor expansion (
4.87
).

Step 1
We will show here that

\|\mathcal{R}_{m_{0}+h,z}\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\sigma})}\leq C(\sigma)(|h|+|z-z_{0}|)\,

(4.90)

for every
\sigma\in]0,1[
, where
\|L\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\sigma})}
is the operator norm of a bounded linear operator
L
on
C^{\sigma}
.
The estimate will have the following consequence. First of all using (
4.84
) and
\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=1
we expand

H(h,z)=1-\langle T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m_{0}+h,z}(\psi_{0}),\psi_{0}\rangle+\underbrace{\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\langle(-T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m_{0}+h,z})^{k}(\psi_{0}),\psi_{0}\rangle}_{=:R_{1}(z,h)}\,.

(4.91)

Hence using (
4.90
) we estimate

\displaystyle|R_{1}(z,h)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\|(-T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{R}_{m_{0}+h,z})^{k}(\psi_{0})\|_{\infty}\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}(\|T^{-1}\|_{C^{\sigma}}\|\mathcal{R}_{m_{0}+h,z}\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\sigma})})^{k}\|\psi_{0}\|_{C^{\sigma}}\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle=o(|h|+|z-z_{0}|)\,,

(4.92)

for some fixed
\sigma
.
In order to show (
4.90
) we write

\mathcal{R}_{m_{0}+h,z}(\psi)=(z-z_{0})\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\Big(\frac{1}{\Xi-z}\Big(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}\psi\Big)\Big)+(2m_{0}h+h^{2})\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}(\psi)\,.

Since
\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{0}}
is smooth, it suffices to show that the operators
B_{z}:=\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\circ\frac{1}{\Xi-z}
are uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{L}(C^{\sigma})
. We first fix a smooth cut-off function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(]a-2,a+2[)
which equals
1
on
[a-1,a+1]
and write

B_{z}=B_{z}^{1}+B_{z}^{2}:=\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\circ\Big(\frac{1-\varphi}{\Xi-z}\Big)+\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\circ\Big(\frac{\varphi}{\Xi-z}\Big)\,.

But since
(1-\varphi)/(\Xi-z)
enjoys a uniform bound in
C^{k}
, it is easy to conclude that
\|B^{1}_{z}\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\sigma})}
is bounded uniformly in
z
. We thus need to bound

\displaystyle B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m_{0}}\int e^{-m_{0}|t-s|}\frac{\varphi(s)}{\Xi(s)-z}\psi(s)\,ds\,.

We first bound
\|B^{2}_{z}\|_{L^{\infty}}
. We write
z=x+iy
and, since
x
is close to
a
, we select the only
a^{\prime}
such that
\Xi(a^{\prime})=x
and write

\displaystyle B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m_{0}}\underbrace{\int e^{-m_{0}|t-s|}\frac{\varphi(s)(\psi(s)-\psi(a^{\prime}))}{(\Xi(s)-\Xi(a^{\prime}))-iy}\,ds}_{=:I_{1}(t)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\psi(a^{\prime})}{2m_{0}}\underbrace{\int e^{-m_{0}|t-s|}\frac{\varphi(s)}{\Xi(s)-z}\,ds}_{=:I_{2}(t)}

Writing
\frac{1}{\Xi-z}=\frac{1}{\Xi^{\prime}}\frac{d}{dt}\ln(\Xi-z)
we can integrate by parts to get

\displaystyle I_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{\int m_{0}\frac{t-s}{|t-s|}e^{-m_{0}|t-s|}(\Xi^{\prime}(s))^{-1}\ln(\Xi(s)-z)\varphi(s)\,ds}_{=:I_{2,1}(t)}

\displaystyle\qquad-\underbrace{\int e^{-m_{0}|t-s|}\ln(\Xi(s)-z)\frac{d}{ds}((\Xi^{\prime})^{-1}\varphi)(s)\,ds}_{=:I_{2,2}(t)}

and use the uniform bound for
\ln(\Xi(s)-z)
in
L^{1}([a-2,a+2])
to conclude that
|I_{2,1}|
and
|I_{2,2}|
are both bounded uniformly. As for
I_{1}
, note that, on any compact interval
K
around
a^{\prime}
, we have, since
\Xi^{\prime}
is continuous and
\Xi^{\prime}<0
,

C(K):=\inf_{x\in K}|\Xi^{\prime}(x)|=-\max_{x\in K}\Xi^{\prime}(x)>0.

Therefore by the mean value theorem, for all
s\in K
, there exists a
\iota=\iota(s)\in K
such that

\displaystyle\lvert\Xi(s)-\Xi(a^{\prime})-iy\rvert

\displaystyle=\sqrt{y^{2}+(\Xi(s)-\Xi(a^{\prime}))^{2}}

\displaystyle>\lvert\Xi(s)-\Xi(a^{\prime})\rvert=\lvert s-a^{\prime}\rvert\lvert\Xi^{\prime}(\iota)\rvert\geq\lvert s-a^{\prime}\rvert C(K).

By the definition of the Hölder semi-norm, we thus have, for all
s\in K
,

\left|\frac{\psi(s)-\psi(a^{\prime})}{\Xi(s)-\Xi(a^{\prime})-iy}\right|\leq\frac{\|\psi\|_{C^{\sigma}}}{C(K)|s-a^{\prime}|^{1-\sigma}},

which is integrable. Furthermore, outside of
K
the integrand of
I_{1}
is bounded and decays exponentially, therefore one can uniformly bound
I_{1}
.

We next wish to bound the seminorm

[B^{2}_{z}(\psi)]_{\sigma}:=\sup_{t\neq t^{\prime}}\frac{|B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t)-B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t^{\prime})|}{|t-t^{\prime}|^{\sigma}}\,.

We write

B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t)-B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t^{\prime})=(I_{1}(t)-I_{1}(t^{\prime}))+\psi^{\prime}(a)(I_{2}(t)-I_{2}(t^{\prime}))\,.

Using that
|e^{-m_{0}|t-s|}-e^{-m_{0}|t^{\prime}-s|}|\leq C|t-t^{\prime}|
we can bound

|I_{1}(t)-I_{1}(t^{\prime})|\leq C|t-t^{\prime}|\int|\varphi(s)|\frac{|\psi(s)-\psi(a^{\prime})|}{|\Xi(s)-\Xi(a^{\prime})|}\,ds\leq C\|\psi\|_{C^{\sigma}}|t-t^{\prime}|\,.

Similarly we can write

|I_{2,2}(t)-I_{2,2}(t^{\prime})|\leq C|t-t^{\prime}|\int\left|\ln(\Xi(s)-z)\frac{d}{ds}((\Xi^{\prime})^{-1}\varphi)(s)\right|\,ds\leq C|t-t^{\prime}|\,.

Next denoting the function
(\Xi^{\prime}(s))^{-1}\varphi(s)\ln(\Xi(s)-z)
by
B(s)
we assume
t>t^{\prime}
and write further

\displaystyle I_{2,1}(t)-I_{2,1}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=m_{0}\Big(\underbrace{\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-m_{0}(s-t)}B(s)\,ds-\int_{t^{\prime}}^{\infty}e^{-m_{0}(s-t^{\prime})}B(s)\,ds}_{=:J_{+}(t,t^{\prime})}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-m_{0}\Big(\underbrace{\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-m_{0}(t-s)}B(s)\,ds-\int_{-\infty}^{t^{\prime}}e^{-m_{0}(t^{\prime}-s)}B(s)\,ds}_{=:J_{-}(t,t^{\prime})}\Big)\,.

Then we choose
p=\frac{1}{\sigma}
, let
p^{\prime}
be the dual exponent and estimate

\displaystyle|J_{+}(t,t^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq C|t-t^{\prime}|\int_{t}^{\infty}|B(s)|\,ds+\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}|B(s)|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq C|t-t^{\prime}|\|B\|_{L^{1}}+|t-t^{\prime}|^{\sigma}\|B\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\,.

A similar estimate for
J_{-}(t,t^{\prime})
finally shows the existence of a constant
C
such that

|B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t)-B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t^{\prime})|\leq C\|\psi\|_{C^{\sigma}}\left(|t-t^{\prime}|+|t-t^{\prime}|^{\sigma}\right)\,.

Clearly this implies

|B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t)-B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t^{\prime})|\leq C\|\psi\|_{C^{\sigma}}|t-t^{\prime}|^{\sigma}\qquad\mbox{if $|t-t^{\prime}|\leq 1$.}

On the other hand we can trivially bound

|B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t)-B^{2}_{z}(\psi)(t^{\prime})|\leq 2\|B^{2}_{z}(\psi)\|_{\infty}\leq C\|\psi\|_{C^{\sigma}}|t-t^{\prime}|^{\sigma}\quad\mbox{if $|t-t^{\prime}|\geq 1$.}

Step 2.
In this second step we compute

\displaystyle\langle T^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{m,z}(\psi_{0}),\psi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\left(A((\Xi-z)^{-1}-(\Xi-z_{0})^{-1})\psi_{0}\right),\psi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+(2m_{0}h+h^{2})\langle T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}(\psi_{0}),\psi_{0}\rangle\,.

Recalling (
4.82
) (and using that
\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}
is self-adjoint) we rewrite the expression as

\displaystyle\langle T^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{m,z}(\psi_{0}),\psi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=(z-z_{0})\langle T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\big(A(\Xi-z)^{-1}(\Xi-z_{0})^{-1}\psi_{0}\big),\psi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+2m_{a}h+h^{2}

\displaystyle=(z-z_{0})\langle A(\Xi-z)^{-1}(\Xi-z_{0})^{-1}\psi_{0},\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\circ(T^{*})^{-1}(\psi_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+2ma_{h}+h^{2}

\displaystyle=(z-z_{0})\underbrace{\langle A(\Xi-z)^{-1}(\Xi-z_{0})^{-1}\psi_{0},\psi_{0}\rangle}_{=:G(z)}+2m_{a}h+h^{2}\,.

(4.93)

We thus want to show that the following limit exists and to compute its imaginary part:

\displaystyle-c(a)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{\textrm{Im}\,z>0,z\to\Xi(a)}G(z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\textrm{Im}\,z>0,z\to\Xi(a)}\int\frac{1}{\Xi(s)-z}|\psi_{0}(s)|^{2}\frac{A(s)}{\Xi(s)-\Xi(a)}\,ds\,.

(4.94)

Observe indeed that inserting
G(z)=-c(a)+o(1)
in (
4.93
) and taking into account (
4.91
) and (
4.92
) we conclude that (
4.87
) holds.

In order to compute
c(a)
we observe first an

\phi(s):=|\psi_{0}(s)|^{2}\frac{A(s)}{\Xi(s)-\Xi(a)}

is smooth and decays exponentially. We thus rewrite

G(z)=\int\frac{1}{\Xi(s)-z}\phi(s)\,ds\,.

Next we decompose
z
into its real and imaginary part as
z=x+iy
and observe that

\displaystyle\lim_{\textrm{Im}\,z>0,z\to\Xi(a)}\textrm{Re}\,G(z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\Xi(a),y\downarrow 0}\int\frac{\Xi(s)-x}{(\Xi(s)-x)^{2}+y^{2}}\phi(s)\,ds

Here we are only interested in showing that the limit exists and we thus fix a cut-off function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(]a-2,a+2[)
, identically
1
on
[a-1,a+1]
and split the integral into

\displaystyle\textrm{Re}\,G(z)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\Xi(s)-x}{(\Xi(s)-x)^{2}+y^{2}}\phi(s)\varphi(s)\,ds

\displaystyle\qquad+\int\frac{\Xi(s)-x}{(\Xi(s)-x)^{2}+y^{2}}\phi(s)(1-\varphi(s))\,ds\,.

The second integral has a limit, while in order to show that the first has a limit we write

\frac{\Xi(s)-x}{(\Xi(s)-x)^{2}+y^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\Xi^{\prime}(s)}\frac{d}{ds}\ln((\Xi(s)-x)^{2}+y^{2})\,.

We then integrate by parts and use the fact that
\ln((\Xi(s)-x)^{2}+y^{2})
converges to
2\ln|(\Xi(s)-\Xi(a)|
strongly in
L^{q}([a-2,a+2])
for every
q
to infer the existence of the limit of the first integral.

As for the imaginary part we write instead

\displaystyle\lim_{\textrm{Im}\,z>0,z\to\Xi(a)}\textrm{Im}\,G(z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\Xi(a),y\downarrow 0}\int\frac{y}{(\Xi(s)-x)^{2}+y^{2}}\phi(s)\,ds\,.

(4.95)

We wish to show that the latter integral converges to

I=\phi(a)\int\frac{ds}{(\Xi^{\prime}(a))^{2}s^{2}+1}=\frac{\pi\phi(a)}{|\Xi^{\prime}(a)|}\,.

(4.96)

On the other hand
\phi(a)=|\psi_{0}(a)|^{2}A^{\prime}(a)(\Xi^{\prime}(a))^{-1}
.
Since
A^{\prime}(a)>0
and
\Xi^{\prime}(a)<0
, we conclude that
c(a)
exists and it is a complex number with positive imaginary part, which completes the proof of the lemma.

It remains to show the convergence of (
4.95
) to (
4.96
). First observe that for each
x
sufficiently close to
\Xi(a)
there is a unique
a^{\prime}=\Xi^{-1}(x)
such that
\Xi(a^{\prime})=x
. Changing variables (
s
becomes
a^{\prime}+s
), the integral in (
4.95
) becomes

\int\frac{y}{(\Xi(a^{\prime}+s)-x)^{2}+y^{2}}\phi(a^{\prime}+s)\,ds\,

(4.97)

and we wish to show that its limit is
I
as
(a^{\prime},y)\to(a,0)
.
Next, fix any
\delta>0
and observe that

\lim_{y\to 0}\int_{|s|\geq\delta}\frac{y}{(\Xi(a^{\prime}+s)-x)^{2}+y^{2}}\phi(a^{\prime}+s)\,ds=0

uniformly in
a^{\prime}\in[a-1,a+1]
. We therefore define

I(\delta,a^{\prime},y):=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\frac{y}{(\Xi(a^{\prime}+s)-x)^{2}+y^{2}}\phi(a^{\prime}+s)\,ds

and we wish to show that, for every
\varepsilon>0
there is a
\delta>0
such that

\limsup_{(a^{\prime},y)\downarrow(a,0)}\left|I(\delta,a^{\prime},y)-I\right|\leq C\varepsilon\,,

(4.98)

where
C
is a geometric constant.
We rewrite

I(\delta,a^{\prime},y)=\int_{-\delta y^{-1}}^{\delta y^{-1}}\frac{\phi(a^{\prime}+ys)}{y^{-2}(\Xi(a^{\prime}+ys)-\Xi(a^{\prime}))^{2}+1}\,ds\,.

Fix now
\varepsilon
and observe that, since
\Xi^{\prime}
and
\phi
are continuous, if
\delta
is chosen sufficiently small, then

\displaystyle((\Xi^{\prime}(a))^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})s^{2}\leq y^{-2}(\Xi(a^{\prime}+ys)-\Xi(a^{\prime}))^{2}\leq((\Xi^{\prime}(a))^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})s^{2}

(4.99)

\displaystyle|\phi(a^{\prime}+ys)-\phi(a)|\leq\varepsilon\,.

(4.100)

for all
|a^{\prime}-a|<\delta
and
y|s|\leq\delta
. Choosing
\varepsilon>0
so that
\varepsilon\leq\frac{|\Xi^{\prime}(a)|}{2}
we easily see that, when
|a^{\prime}-a|<\delta
, we have

\left|I(\delta,a^{\prime},y)-\phi(a)\int_{-\delta y^{-1}}^{\delta y^{-1}}\frac{ds}{(\Xi^{\prime}(a))^{2}s^{2}+1}\right|\leq C\varepsilon\,.

In particular, as
y\downarrow 0
, we conclude (
4.98
).
∎

4.9.
Proof of Proposition
4.4.5

We reduce the proof of Proposition
4.4.5
to the following lemma.

Lemma 4.9.1
.

Consider
G:=\{m>1,m\neq m_{a},m_{b}:\mathscr{U}_{m}\neq\emptyset\}
. Then
G
is relatively open and relatively closed in
]1,\infty[\setminus\{m_{a},m_{b}\}
.

Proposition
4.4.5
is an obvious consequence of the latter lemma and of Proposition
4.4.3
: Lemma
4.9.1
implies that
G
is the union of connected components of
[1,\infty[\setminus\{m_{a},m_{b}\}
. On the other hand the connected component
]m_{b},m_{a}[
intersects
G
because of Proposition
4.4.3
and thus it is contained in
G
.
We thus complete the proof of Proposition
4.4.5
showing Lemma
4.9.1

Proof of Lemma
4.9.1
.

We start with some preliminary considerations. Fix an interval
[c,d]\subset]1,\infty[\setminus\{m_{a},m_{b}\}
.
Recalling Proposition
4.1.4
we know that, since the operator norm of
\mathcal{L}_{m}
is bounded uniformly in
m\in[c,d]
,

(a)

There is
R>0
such that
\mathscr{U}_{m}\subset B_{R}(0)
for all
m\in[c,d]
.

However it also follows from Proposition
4.4.1
that

(b)

There is a
\delta>0
such that
\mathscr{U}_{m}\subset\{\textrm{Im}\,z>\delta\}
.

Step 1.
We first prove that
G
is relatively closed. To that end we fix a sequence
m_{j}\to m\in]1,\infty[\setminus\{m_{a},m_{b}\}
such that
m_{j}
belongs to
G
. Without loss of generality we can assume
\{m_{j}\}\subset[c,d]\subset]1,\infty[\setminus\{m_{a},m_{b}\}
. For each
m_{j}
we can then consider
z_{j}\in\mathscr{U}_{m_{j}}
, which by (a) and (b) we can assume to converge to some
z\in\mathbb{C}
with positive imaginary part. We then let
\psi_{j}
be a sequence of nontrivial elements in
L^{2}
such that

-\psi_{j}^{\prime\prime}+m_{j}^{2}\psi_{j}+\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\psi_{j}=0\,,

(4.101)

and normalize them to
\|\psi_{j}\|_{L^{2}}=1

Since
\textrm{Im}\,z_{j}\geq\delta>0
, the sequence of functions
\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}
enjoy uniform bounds in the spaces
L^{1}
and
C^{k}
. We can then argue as in Section
4.6
to find that

(i)

\|\psi_{j}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}}
enjoy a uniform bound;

(ii)

There are uniformly bounded nonzero constants
\{C^{\pm}_{j}\}
with the property that
\psi_{j}
is asymptotic to
C^{\pm}_{j}e^{\mp m_{j}t}
and
\pm\infty
;

(iii)

There is a
T_{0}>0
independent of
j
with the property that

|\psi_{j}(t)-C^{\pm}_{j}e^{\mp m_{j}t}|\leq\frac{|C^{\pm}_{j}|}{2}e^{\mp m_{j}t}\qquad\forall\pm t>T_{0}\,.

These three properties together imply that a subsequence, not relabeled, converges strongly in
L^{2}
to some
\psi
. Passing into the limit in (
4.101
) we conclude that

-\psi^{\prime\prime}+m^{2}\psi+\frac{A}{\Xi-z}\psi=0\,.

This shows that
z\in\mathscr{U}_{m}
, i.e. that
m\in G
.

Step 2.
Here we show that
G
is relatively open. To that end we consider some sequence
m_{j}\to m\in]1,\infty[\setminus\{m_{a},m_{b}\}
with the property that
m_{j}\not\in G
and we show that
m\not\in G
. By (a) and (b) above, it suffices to show that the domain

\Delta:=\{|z|<R:\textrm{Im}\,z>\delta\}

does not contain any element of
\textrm{spec}\,(\mathcal{L}_{m})
. Observe first that, since we know that it does not intersect
\gamma=\partial\Delta
, the distance between
\gamma
and any element in
\textrm{spec}\,(\mathcal{L}_{m})
is larger than a positive constant
\varepsilon
. Recalling that the spectrum on the upper half complex space is discrete, we have that

P_{m}:=\int_{\gamma}(\mathcal{L}_{m}-z)^{-1}\,dz

is a projection on a finite-dimensional space which contains all eigenspaces of the elements
z\in\textrm{spec}\,(\mathcal{L}_{m})\cap\Delta=m\mathscr{U}_{m}=\{mz:z\in\mathscr{U}_{m}\}
. And since all such elements belong to the discrete spectrum,
\mathscr{U}_{m}=\emptyset
if and only if
P_{m}=0
. On the other hand

P_{m_{j}}:=\int_{\gamma}(\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}-z)^{-1}\,dz

equals
0
precisely because
m_{j}\not\in G
. We thus just need to show that
P_{m_{j}}
converges to
P_{m}
to infer that
m\in G
. The latter follows from the following observations:

(i)

Since
\gamma
is a compact set and does not intersect the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{m}
, there is a constant
M
such that
\|(\mathcal{L}_{m}-z)^{-1}\|_{O}\leq M
for all
z\in\gamma
;

(ii)

\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}
converges to
\mathcal{L}_{m}
in the operator norm;

(iii)

Writing

\displaystyle\qquad(\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}-z)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\textrm{Id}+(\mathcal{L}_{m}-z)^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}-\mathcal{L}_{m}))^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{m}-z)^{-1}\,,

when
\|\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}-\mathcal{L}_{m}\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\frac{1}{2M}
we can use the Neumann series for the inverse to infer

\sup_{z\in\gamma}\|(\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}-z)^{-1}-(\mathcal{L}_{m}-z)^{-1}\|_{O}\leq C\|\mathcal{L}_{m}-\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}\|_{O}\,,

for some constant
C
independent of
j
.

We then conclude that
P_{m_{j}}
converges to
P_{m}
in the operator norm.
∎

Remark 4.9.2
.

An immediate outcome of the argument above is that the sum of the algebraic multiplicities of
z\in\mathscr{U}_{m}
, as eigenvalues of
m^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m}
, is constant on any connected component of
]\!-\!\infty,\infty[\setminus\{m_{a},m_{b}\}
. Indeed, it coincides with the rank of the operator
P_{m}
defined in Step 2.

4.10.
Proof of Lemma
4.4.7

We start by observing that

\Xi^{\prime}(t):=\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-2(t-\tau)}A(\tau)\,d\tau\,.

In fact, since
A=\Xi^{\prime\prime}+2\Xi
, the identity follows from the classical solution formula for first order ODEs with constant coefficients once we show that the right hand side and the left hand side coincide for all sufficiently negative
t
. The latter can be verified with a direct computation using that
\Xi^{\prime}(t)=-2c_{0}2^{2t}
for all
t
sufficiently negative.

We next read the conditions for
\Xi\in\mathscr{C}
in terms of
A
to find that it suffices to impose

(i)

A(t)=-8c_{0}e^{2t}
for all
t
sufficiently negative;

(ii)

A(t)=-{\bar{\alpha}}e^{-{\bar{\alpha}}t}
for all
t\geq\ln 2
;

(iii)

There are exactly two zeros
a<b
of
A
and
A^{\prime}(a)>0
,
A^{\prime}(b)<0
;

(iv)

\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-2(t-\tau)}A(\tau)d\tau<0
for every
t
.

Note indeed that, by imposing
\Xi(\infty)=0
, with a simple computation we can determine
\Xi(-\infty)
as

\Xi(-\infty)=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Xi^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}A(\tau)\,d\tau\,

and moreover (using
\Xi^{\prime}<0
, which follows from (iv)) we conclude that the conditions (i)-(iv) ensure

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}A(\tau)\,d\tau<0\,.

We next fix
a=0
and
b=\frac{1}{2}
and rather than imposing (iv) we impose the two conditions

(v)

\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)d\tau=-1
;

(vi)

\max A\leq\frac{1}{e}
.

Observe, indeed, that (iv) is equivalent to

\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau<0

and that, since
A
is negative on
]-\infty,0[
and
]\frac{1}{2},\infty[
, the integral on the left-hand side is maximal for
t=\frac{1}{2}
. We then can use (v) and (vi) to estimate

\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau\leq-1+\frac{e}{2}\max A\leq-\frac{1}{2}\,.

We next recall that, by the Rayleigh criterion,

-\lambda_{a}=\min_{\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}=1}\langle\psi,L_{a}\psi\rangle=\min_{\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}=1}\int\Big(|\psi^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(0)}|\psi|^{2}\Big)\,.

We test the right-hand side with

\psi(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{for $|t|\geq\frac{1}{2}$}\\
\\
\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi t)&\mbox{for $|t|\leq\frac{1}{2}$.}\end{array}\right.

We therefore get

-\lambda_{a}\leq 2\pi^{2}+2\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}\frac{A(t)}{\Xi(t)-\Xi(0)}\cos^{2}\pi t\,dt\,.

(4.102)

Next, for any fixed positive constant
B>0
, we impose that
A(t)=Bt
on the interval
]-\sqrt{B}^{-1},0]
and we then continue it smoothly on
[0,\infty[
so to satisfy (ii), (iii), and (v) on
[0,\infty[
(the verification that this is possible is rather simple). We also can continue it smoothly on
]\!-\!\infty,-\sqrt{B}^{-1}]
so to ensure (i). In order to ensure (v) as well we just need to show that

\int_{-\sqrt{B}^{-1}}^{0}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau\geq-\frac{1}{2}\,.

The latter is certainly ensured by

\int_{-\sqrt{B}^{-1}}^{0}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau\geq\int_{-\sqrt{B}^{-1}}^{0}A(\tau)\,d\tau=-\frac{1}{2}\,.

Now, observe that
\Xi^{\prime}(0)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau=-1
. For
t\in\,]-\sqrt{B}^{-1},0[
we wish to estimate
\Xi^{\prime}(t)
and to do it we compute

\displaystyle|\Xi^{\prime}(t)-\Xi^{\prime}(0)|

\displaystyle=\left|e^{-2t}\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau-\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau\right|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2t}\left|\int_{t}^{0}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau\right|+(e^{-2t}-1)\left|\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{2\tau}A(\tau)\,d\tau\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{2\sqrt{B}^{-1}}}{2}+(e^{2\sqrt{B}^{-1}}-1)\leq\frac{3}{4}\,,

which can be ensured by taking
B
sufficiently large. In particular
-\frac{1}{4}\geq\Xi^{\prime}(t)\geq-2
for
t\in\,]-\sqrt{B}^{-1},0[
. We thus conclude that

-2t\leq\Xi(t)-\Xi(0)\leq-\frac{t}{4}\qquad\forall t\in\,]-\sqrt{B}^{-1},0[\,.

In turn the latter can be used to show

\frac{A(t)}{\Xi(t)-\Xi(0)}\leq-\frac{B}{2}\qquad\forall t\in\,]-\sqrt{B}^{-1},0[\,.

Since
\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(0)}
is otherwise negative on
]-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}[
, we conclude

-\lambda_{a}\leq 2\pi^{2}-2\int_{-\sqrt{B}^{-1}}^{0}B\cos^{2}\pi t\,dt\,.

(4.103)

By taking
B
large enough we can ensure that
\cos^{2}\pi t\geq\frac{1}{2}
on the interval
]-\sqrt{B}^{-1},0[
. In particular we achieve

-\lambda_{a}\leq 2\pi^{2}-\sqrt{B}\,.

Since we can choose
\sqrt{B}
as large as we wish, the latter inequality completes the proof of the lemma.

Chapter 5
Nonlinear theory

This final chapter will prove Theorem
2.5.1
and hence complete the argument leading to Theorem
1.0.1
. To that end we fix a choice of
\bar{\Omega}
,
\bar{V}
,
m
and
\eta
as given by Theorem
2.4.2
, where
\bar{a}>0
is a large parameter whose choice will be specified only later. We moreover fix a suitable subspace
X
of
L^{2}_{m}
and we will prove an estimate corresponding to (
2.46
) in this smaller space.

Definition 5.0.1
.

We denote by
X
the subspace of elements
\Omega\in L^{2}_{m}
for which the following norm is finite:

\|\Omega\|_{X}:=\|\Omega\|_{L^{2}}+\||x|\nabla\Omega\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\Omega\|_{L^{4}}\,.

(5.1)

The above norm has two features which will play a crucial role in our estimates. The first feature is that it ensures an appropriate decay of the
L^{2}
norm of
D\Omega
on the complements of large disks
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R}
. The second feature is that it allows to bound the
L^{\infty}
norm of
\Omega
and
\nabla(K_{2}*\Omega)
and to give a bound on the growth of
K_{2}*\Omega
at infinity. More precisely, we have the following:

Proposition 5.0.2
.

For all
\kappa\in]0,1[
, there is a constant
C(\kappa)>0
such that the following estimates hold for every
m
-fold symmetric
\Omega\in X
:

\displaystyle|\nabla(K_{2}*\Omega)(x)|+|\Omega(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(\kappa)}{1+|x|^{1-\kappa}}\|\Omega\|_{X}\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

(5.2)

\displaystyle|K_{2}*\Omega(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}\min\{|x|,1\}\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,.

(5.3)

The aim of this chapter is therefore to give the bound

\|\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{X}\leq e^{\tau(a_{0}+\delta_{0})}\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\tau\leq\tau_{0}\,

(5.4)

for some appropriately chosen constants
\delta_{0}>0
and
\tau_{0}<0
, independent of
k
. Of course the main difficulty will be to give the explicit estimate (
5.4
). However a first point will be to show that the norm is indeed finite for every
\tau\geq-k
. This will be a consequence of the following:

Lemma 5.0.3
.

Provided
a_{0}
is large enough, the eigenfunction
\eta
of Theorem
2.4.2
belongs to
C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\})
and satisfies the pointwise estimates

|D^{j}\eta(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{-m-2-j-{\bar{\alpha}}}\qquad\forall x,\forall j\in\{0,1,2\}\,.

(5.5)

In particular
\eta\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and its first derivatives are Lipschitz (namely,
\eta\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
). Moreover
K_{2}*\eta\in L^{2}
.

One relevant outcome of Lemma

5.0.3
is that the bound (
5.4
) holds at least for
\tau
sufficiently close to
-k
, given that
\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,-k)\equiv 0
. The main point of (
5.4
) is then that we will be able to deduce the following estimates.

Lemma 5.0.4
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.5.1
there is a constant
C_{0}
(independent of
k
) such that the following holds. Assume that
\bar{\tau}\leq 0
is such that for all
\tau\in[-k,\bar{\tau}]
we have the estimate

\|\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{X}\leq e^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}.

(5.6)

Then

\displaystyle\|\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,\bar{\tau})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}e^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\bar{\tau}}\,,

(5.7)

\displaystyle\||x|D\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,\bar{\tau})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}e^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\bar{\tau}}\,,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\|D\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,\bar{\tau})\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}e^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\bar{\tau}}\,.

(5.9)

With the above lemma we easily conclude (
5.4
) (and hence Theorem
2.5.1
). Indeed denote by
\tau_{k}
the largest non-positive time such that

\|\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{X}\leq e^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\qquad\forall\tau\in[-k,\tau_{k}]\,.

(5.10)

Then we must have

\|\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,\tau_{k})\|_{X}=e^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau_{k}}\,.

(5.11)

On the other hand, summing the three estimates (
5.7
), (
5.8
), and (
5.9
) we conclude

\|\Omega_{\text{per},k}(\cdot,\tau_{k})\|_{X}\leq\bar{C}e^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau_{k}}

(5.12)

for some constant
\bar{C}
independent of
k
. However (
5.11
) and (
5.12
) give

e^{\delta_{0}\tau_{k}}\geq\bar{C}^{-1}
, i.e.
\tau_{k}\geq-\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}\ln\bar{C}
, implying that (
5.4
) holds with
\tau_{0}:=-\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}\ln\bar{C}
.

After proving Proposition
5.0.2
and Lemma
5.0.3
, we will dedicate two separate sections to the three estimates (
5.7
), (
5.8
), and (
5.9
). The first estimate, which we will call
baseline estimate
, will differ substantially from the other two, and to it we will dedicate a section. In order to accomplish the gain in the exponent in (
5.7
) we will use crucially the information on the semigroup which comes from Theorem
2.4.2
and Theorem
3.0.4
, namely, that the growth bound
\omega(L_{ss})
is precisely
a_{0}
(i.e., the growth achieved by
\Omega_{\text{lin}}
). Since, however, the terms in the Duhamel’s formula depend on derivatives, we need to invoke an a priori control of them, which is present in the norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
. Indeed, one such term experiencing the derivative loss arise from the nonlinearity is the following:

\int_{-k}^{\tau}e^{(\tau-s)L_{\textrm{ss}}}[(K_{2}*\Omega_{\text{per},k})\cdot\nabla\Omega_{\text{per},k}](\cdot,s)\,ds\,.

(5.13)

Note that
\|\cdot\|_{X}
also includes the weighted
L^{2}
norm
\||x|D\Omega\|_{L^{2}}
because we encounter a term where
D\Omega_{\text{per},k}
is multiplied by the function
V^{r}
, which grows at
\infty
like
|x|^{1-{\bar{\alpha}}}
when
{\bar{\alpha}}\in]0,2[
. In order to close the argument we then need to control the
L^{4}
norm and the weighted
L^{2}
norm of
D\Omega_{\text{per},k}
. The latter estimates will not be accomplished through a growth bound on the semigroup
e^{\tau L_{ss}}
(which would invoke controls on yet higher derivatives), but rather through some careful energy estimates. The structure of the problem will then enter crucially, since the term
(K_{2}*\Omega_{\text{per},k})\cdot\nabla\bar{\Omega}
which we need to bound in the energy estimates will take advantage of the improved baseline estimate on the
L^{2}
norm. The above term,
which is responsible for the creation of unstable eigenvalues, actually
gains a derivative
. Finally, there is one remaining difficulty when estimating
D\Omega_{\text{per},k}
due to the transport term. Namely, differentiating the equation in cartesian coordinates contributes a term
(\partial_{i}\bar{V})\cdot\nabla\Omega_{\text{per},k}
, which could destabilize the estimates. We exploit the structure of the problem again in a crucial way by estimating angular and radial derivatives, rather than derivatives in cartesian coordinates, and by estimating the angular derivatives
first
and the radial derivatives
after
.

5.1.
Proof of Proposition
5.0.2

We start by bounding
|\Omega(x)|
. Since
W^{1,4}(B_{2})
embeds in
C^{1/2}
, the bound
|\Omega(x)|\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}
is true for every
x\in B_{2}
. Consider further
R:=\frac{|x|}{2}\geq 1
and define
u_{R}(y):=\Omega(Ry)
. In particular let
B:=B_{1}(\frac{x}{R})
and notice that

\displaystyle\|u_{R}\|_{L^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle=R^{-1}\|\Omega\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(x))}\leq R^{-1}\|\Omega\|_{X}\,,

(5.14)

\displaystyle\|Du_{R}\|_{L^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle=\|D\Omega\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(x))}\leq R^{-1}\||\cdot|D\Omega\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(x))}\leq R^{-1}\|\Omega\|_{X}\,,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\|Du_{R}\|_{L^{4}(B)}

\displaystyle=R^{1/2}\|D\Omega\|_{L^{4}(B_{R}(x))}\leq R^{1/2}\|\Omega\|_{X}\,.

(5.16)

By interpolation, for
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{\lambda}{2}+\frac{1-\lambda}{4}
we have

\|Du_{R}\|_{L^{p}(B)}\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}R^{-\lambda+(1-\lambda)/2}\,.

Choosing
p
very close to
2
, but larger, we achieve
\|Du_{R}\|_{L^{p}(B)}\leq CR^{-1+\kappa}\|\Omega\|_{X}
. Since the average of
u_{R}
over
B
is smaller than
\|u_{R}\|_{L^{2}(B)}\leq CR^{-1}\|\Omega\|_{X}
, from Poincaré we conclude
\|u_{R}\|_{W^{1,p}(B)}\leq CR^{-1+\kappa}\|\Omega\|_{X}
. In particular using the embedding of
W^{1,p}
in
L^{\infty}
we conclude

\|u_{R}\|_{L^{\infty}(B)}\leq C(p)R^{-1+\kappa}\|\Omega\|_{X}\,.

(5.17)

Since however
|\Omega(x)|\leq\|u_{R}\|_{L^{\infty}(B)}
, we reach the estimate

|\Omega(x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{1-\kappa}}\|\Omega\|_{X}\,.

(5.18)

Note that the constant
C
depends on
\kappa
:
\kappa
is positive, but a very small choice of it forces to choose a
p
very close to
2
, which in turn gives a dependence of the constant
C(p)
in (
5.17
) on
\kappa
.

We next come to the estimates for
\nabla K_{2}*\Omega
. First of all, observe that

\displaystyle\|\nabla K_{2}*\Omega\|_{L^{2}}\leq

\displaystyle C\|\Omega\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}

\displaystyle\|D^{2}K_{2}*\Omega\|_{L^{2}}+\|D^{2}K_{2}*\Omega\|_{L^{4}}\leq

\displaystyle C\|D\Omega\|_{L^{2}}+C\|D\Omega\|_{L^{4}}\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}

and thus
|\nabla K_{2}*\Omega(x)|\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}
follows for every
x\in B_{4}
. Consider now
|x|\geq 4
, set
R:=\frac{|x|}{4}
and let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{2R}(x))
be a cut-off function identically equal to
1
on
B_{R}(x)
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{2R})
equal to
1
on
B_{R}
. We choose them so that
\|D^{k}\psi\|_{C^{0}}+\|D^{k}\varphi\|_{C^{0}}\leq C(k)R^{-k}
.

We split

\nabla K_{2}*\Omega=\nabla K_{2}*(\varphi\Omega)+\nabla K_{2}*(\psi\Omega)+\nabla K_{2}*((1-\varphi-\psi)\Omega)\,=:F_{1}+F_{2}+F_{3}\,.

We have

\displaystyle\|F_{1}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\varphi\Omega\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}

\displaystyle\|DF_{1}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|D(\varphi\Omega)\|_{L^{2}}\leq CR^{-1}\|\Omega\|_{L^{2}}+C\|\varphi D\Omega\|_{L^{2}}\leq CR^{-1}\|\Omega\|_{X}

\displaystyle\|DF_{1}\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}\,.

The argument used above implies then
|F_{1}(x)|\leq C(\kappa)|x|^{\kappa-1}\|\Omega\|_{X}
. As for estimating
F_{2}
we observe that
F_{2}
is harmonic outside
B_{2R}
. On the other hand
\|F_{2}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}
. Using the mean-value inequality for harmonic functions we then get

|F_{2}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{\pi(2R)^{2}}\int_{B_{2R}(x)}|F_{2}|\leq\frac{C}{R}\|\Omega\|_{X}\,.

As for
F_{3}
we write, using the bound on
|\Omega|
and
|\nabla K(x-y)|\leq C|x-y|^{-2}
,

\displaystyle|F_{3}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus(B_{2R}(x)\cup B_{2R})}\frac{C(\kappa)\|\Omega\|_{X}}{|x-y|^{2}|y|^{1-\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{2R})\cap\{|x-y|\geq|y|\}}\frac{C(\kappa)\|\Omega\|_{X}}{|y|^{3-\kappa}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{2R}(x))\cap\{|y|\geq|x-y|\}}\frac{C(\kappa)\|\Omega\|_{X}}{|x-y|^{3-\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(\kappa)\|\Omega\|_{X}}{R^{1-\kappa}}\,.

Recalling that
K_{2}*\Omega(0)=0
, integrating (
5.2
) on the segment with endpoints
0
and
x
we conclude (
5.3
) for
|x|\leq 2
. In order to prove the bound when
|x|\geq 1
, fix a point
y
with
3R:=|y|\geq 1
. Let
\varphi
be a radial cut-off function which is identically equal to
1
on
B_{R}(0)
, is supported in
B_{2R}(0)
and whose gradient is bounded by
CR^{-1}
. We then write

K_{2}*\Omega=K_{2}*(\varphi\Omega)+K_{2}*((1-\varphi)\Omega)\,.

(5.19)

Since the distance between
y
and the support of
\varphi\Omega
is larger than
R
, we can estimate

|K_{2}*(\varphi\Omega)(y)|\leq\frac{C}{R}\int|\varphi\Omega|\leq C\|\Omega\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}\,.

(5.20)

Next observe that, by Calderon-Zygmund,

\displaystyle\|D^{2}K_{2}*(\Omega(1-\varphi))\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\|D((1-\varphi)\Omega)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{R}\|\Omega\|_{L^{2}}+C\|D\Omega\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{R}\|\Omega\|_{X}\,.

Since
(1-\varphi)\Omega
belongs to
L^{2}_{m}
, the average over
B
of
K_{2}*((1-\varphi)\Omega)
equals
0
. Hence we conclude from Poincaré inequality and Calderon-Zygmund that

\displaystyle\|K_{2}*((1-\varphi)\Omega)\|_{L^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle\leq CR\|DK_{2}*((1-\varphi)\Omega)\|_{L^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle\leq CR\|((1-\varphi)\Omega)\|_{L^{2}(B)}\,.

\displaystyle\leq CR\|\Omega\|_{X}\,.

From Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality applied on
B
we have

\displaystyle\|K_{2}*((1-\varphi)\Omega)\|_{L^{\infty}(B)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|D^{2}K_{2}*(\Omega(1-\varphi))\|_{L^{2}}^{1/2}\|K_{2}*((1-\varphi)\Omega)\|^{1/2}_{L^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{R}\|K_{2}*((1-\varphi)\Omega)\|_{L^{2}(B)}\leq C\|\Omega\|_{X}.

5.2.
Proof of Lemma
5.0.3

The proof of the estimates for
\eta
and its derivatives can be found below.

As for the second conclusion of the Lemma, observe that, by going back to the solutions
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}
, it follows from the regularity and decay of the initial data in (
2.20
) (just proved in the above lemma) and the regularity and decay of the forcing term
f
for positive times, that
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}\in C([t_{k},T],X)
for every
T>t_{k}
. Given the explicit transformation from
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}
to
\Omega_{\varepsilon,k}
we conclude that
\Omega_{\varepsilon,k}\in C([-k,T],X)
for every
T>-k
. Since the same regularity is enjoyed by
\tilde{\Omega}
on
[-k,T]
(the latter is in fact smooth and compactly supported on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[-k,T]
) and by
\Omega_{\text{lin}}
, we infer that
\Omega_{\text{per},k}=\Omega_{\varepsilon,k}-\tilde{\Omega}-\Omega_{\text{lin}}
belongs to
C([-k,T],X)
.

Proof of the first part of Lemma
5.0.3
.

Consider
\eta\in L^{2}_{m}
, for
m\geq 2
as in Theorem
2.4.2
and write it as
\eta(\theta,r)=\vartheta(r)e^{ikm\theta}
when
b_{0}\neq 0
or
\eta(\theta,r)=\vartheta(r)e^{ikm\theta}+\bar{\vartheta}(r)e^{-ikm\theta}
if
b_{0}=0
(where
\bar{\vartheta}
denotes the complex conjugate of
\vartheta
).
In both cases
\vartheta(r)e^{ikm\theta}
is an eigenfunction and through this property we will show that it satisfies the estimates of the Lemma. We can therefore assume without loss of generality that
\eta(x)=\vartheta(r)e^{ikm\theta}
. We will also see that the argument leads in fact to estimates (
5.5
) with
km
replacing
m
and hence without loss of generality we assume

k=1
. Furthermore an outcome of the argument below is that
\eta
is smooth except possibly at the origin.
Note moreover that after having shown the pointwise bounds (
5.5
) for
\eta
outside of the origin, the
W^{2,\infty}
regularity of
\eta
follows immediately: indeed
\eta
and
D\eta
can be continuously extended to the origin by setting them equal to
0
, hence showing that
\eta\in C^{1}(B_{1})
, while the uniform bound of
|D^{2}\eta|
in
B_{1}\setminus\{0\}
easily implies that
D\eta
is Lipschitz.

Step 1. Exponential coordinates.
We recall that the distribution
K_{2}*\eta
is well defined, according to Lemma
2.4.1
, and its action on a test function in
\mathscr{S}
is given by integrating the scalar product of the test with a function
v\in W^{1,2}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, cf. the proof of Lemma
2.4.1
in the appendix. It follows from the argument given there that
R_{2\pi/m}v(R_{2\pi/m}x)=v(x)
. Given that
\textrm{div}\,V=0
,
-V^{\perp}
is the gradient of a continuous function, which is determined by its value
\psi
at the origin.
\psi
inherits the symmetry and can thus be written as
\psi(\theta,r)=f(r)e^{im\theta}
. We thus conclude that

\displaystyle\vartheta=

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{r}f^{\prime}-\frac{m^{2}}{r^{2}}f

(5.21)

\displaystyle v=

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime}}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}-\frac{imf}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\,.

(5.22)

Observe moreover that
v\in W^{1,2}_{\text{loc}}
implies
f^{\prime\prime},\frac{f^{\prime}}{r}
,
\frac{f}{r^{2}}\in L^{2}_{\text{loc}}
. Therefore
f
is determined by (
5.21
) and the boundary conditions

(a)

f(0)=0
;

(b)

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|f^{\prime}(r)|^{2}}{r}\,dr<\infty
.

(observe that condition (b) implies
f^{\prime}(0)=0
when
f\in C^{1}
and we can therefore interpret it as a surrogate for a boundary condition). We recall also that we have the estimate
\|D^{2}\psi\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\vartheta\|_{L^{2}}<\infty
and owing to
\int_{B_{R}}D\psi=0
, by Poincaré we achieve

\|D\psi\|_{L^{p}(B_{R})}\leq C(p)R^{2/p}\,.

Using Morrey’s embedding and the fact that
\psi(0)=0
we conclude, in turn

|\psi(x)|\leq C\|D\psi\|_{L^{p}(B_{|x|})}|x|^{1-2/p}\leq C|x|\,.

In particular we conclude

|f(r)|\leq Cr\,.

(5.23)

The equation satisfied by
\eta
can thus be written in terms of the function
f
as

\left(1+\frac{r}{\alpha}\frac{d}{dr}-z_{0}-im\beta\zeta\right)\left(f^{\prime\prime}+\frac{f^{\prime}}{r}-\frac{m^{2}}{r^{2}}f\right)-\frac{imf\beta g^{\prime}}{r}=0\,,

(5.24)

where
g
is the smooth function such that
\bar{\Omega}(x)=g(|x|)
(in particular
g
is constant in a neighborhood of the origin, and equals
r^{-\alpha}
for
r\geq 2
) and
\zeta
is given by the formula (
2.8
). We next set
s=\ln r
and in particular

\displaystyle\tilde{g}(s)

\displaystyle=g(e^{s})

(5.25)

\displaystyle h(s)

\displaystyle=f(e^{s})

(5.26)

\displaystyle\tilde{\zeta}(s)

\displaystyle=\zeta(e^{s})\,.

(5.27)

Note that

\vartheta(e^{s})=e^{-2s}(h^{\prime\prime}(s)-m^{2}h(s))=:e^{-2s}\Gamma(s)\,.

(5.28)

In these new coordinates we observe that the claim of the lemma corresponds then to showing that
\vartheta\in C^{2}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
and

\displaystyle|\Gamma(s)|+|\Gamma^{\prime}(s)|+|\Gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{4s}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall s\leq 0\,,

(5.29)

\displaystyle|\Gamma(s)|+|\Gamma^{\prime}(s)|+|\Gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-(m+{\bar{\alpha}})s}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall s\geq 0\,.

(5.30)

In order to achieve the latter estimates we will need the following bounds on
\tilde{g}^{\prime}
,
\tilde{g}^{\prime\prime}
,
\tilde{\zeta}
, and
\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}
, which we record here and can be easily checked:

\displaystyle|\tilde{g}(s)|+|\tilde{g}^{\prime}(s)|+|\tilde{\zeta}(s)|+|\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-{\bar{\alpha}}s}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall s\geq 0\,,

(5.31)

\displaystyle|\tilde{g}(s)-g(0)|+|\tilde{g}^{\prime}(s)|+|\tilde{\zeta}(s)-\zeta(0)|+|\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{2s}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall s\leq 0\,.

(5.32)

We observe next that, by (
5.23
)

|h(s)|\leq Ce^{s}\,.

(5.33)

Step 2. The equation for
h
.
In these new coordinates the equation (
5.24
) becomes then

\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{d}{ds}-z_{0}-im\beta\tilde{\zeta}\right)\left(e^{-2s}(h^{\prime\prime}-m^{2}h)\right)-imhe^{-2s}\tilde{g}^{\prime}=0\,,

(5.34)

which we simplify as

\left[\frac{d}{ds}-\alpha\left(im\beta\tilde{\zeta}+z_{0}-1+\frac{2}{\alpha}\right)\right](h^{\prime\prime}-m^{2}h)-i\alpha mh\tilde{g}^{\prime}=0\,.

(5.35)

We then define the integrating factor

I(s)=\exp\left[-\alpha\int_{0}^{s}\left(im\beta\tilde{\zeta}+z_{0}-1+\frac{2}{\alpha}\right)\,d\sigma\right]

We can thus write

\frac{d}{ds}\left[I(h^{\prime\prime}-m^{2}h)\right]=i\alpha mIh\tilde{g}^{\prime}\,.

Given that
z_{0}=a_{0}+ib_{0}
,

|I(s)|\leq Ce^{-(2+\alpha(a_{0}-1))s}

(5.36)

and in particular, by (
5.31
)

|Ih\tilde{g}^{\prime}|(s)\leq Ce^{-(1+\alpha a_{0}+{({\bar{\alpha}}-\alpha)})s}\,.

(5.37)

This implies that the latter is an integrable function on every halfline
[s,\infty[
so that we can write

\Gamma(s)=h^{\prime\prime}(s)-m^{2}h(s)=-\alpha I(s)^{-1}\int_{s}^{\infty}imIh\tilde{g}^{\prime}\,.

(5.38)

Since
\Gamma(s)=e^{2s}\vartheta(e^{s})
and
\vartheta\in L^{2}(rdr)
,
e^{-s}\Gamma\in L^{2}(ds)
. We claim in particular that
e^{-m|s|}\Gamma(s)
is integrable. Indeed:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\Gamma(s)|e^{-m|s|}\,ds\leq\|e^{-s}\Gamma\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2(m|s|-s)}\,ds\right)^{1/2}<\infty\,.

We claim then that for the function
h
we have the formula

h(s)=-\frac{1}{2m}e^{-ms}\int_{-\infty}^{s}e^{ms^{\prime}}\Gamma(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2m}e^{ms}\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{-ms^{\prime}}\Gamma(s^{\prime})\,ds^{\prime}\,.

(5.39)

In order to prove the identity denote the right hand side by
H
and observe that it is a solution of the
same ODE satisfied by
H
, namely,
H^{\prime\prime}-m^{2}H=\Gamma
. Hence
(\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}-m^{2})(H-h)=0
, which implies that
H(s)-h(s)=C_{1}e^{ms}+C_{2}e^{-ms}
. On the other hand, using the information that
e^{-s}\Gamma\in L^{2}
, it can be readily checked that
H(s)=o(e^{m|s|})
at both
\pm\infty
. Since this property is shared by
h
, thanks to the bound (
5.33
), we conclude that
C_{1}e^{ms}+C_{2}e^{-ms}
must be
o(e^{m|s|})
at
\pm\infty
, implying
C_{1}=C_{2}=0
.

Step 3. Estimates at
+\infty
.
In this step we give bounds for the asymptotic behavior of
h
at
+\infty
.
We recall (
5.31
) and hence observe that

|\Gamma(s)|\leq Ce^{(2+\alpha a_{0}-\alpha)s}\int_{s}^{\infty}|h(\sigma)|e^{-(2+\alpha a_{0}+{({\bar{\alpha}}-\alpha)})\sigma}\,d\sigma\,,

(5.40)

for
s
positive.
On the other hand, for
s\geq 0
we can also write from (
5.39
)

|h(s)|\leq Ce^{-ms}+Ce^{-ms}\int_{0}^{s}e^{m\sigma}|\Gamma(\sigma)|\,d\sigma+Ce^{ms}\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{-m\sigma}|\Gamma(\sigma)|\,d\sigma\,,

(5.41)

Starting with the information
|h(s)|\leq Ce^{s}
for
s>0
, we then infer from (
5.40
) that
|\Gamma(s)|\leq Ce^{(1-{\bar{\alpha}})s}
for
s>0
. In turn plugging the latter into (
5.41
) we infer
|h(s)|\leq Ce^{(1-{\bar{\alpha}})s}
for
s>0
. The latter, plugged into (
5.40
) turns into
|\Gamma(s)|\leq Ce^{(1-2{\bar{\alpha}})s}
for
s>0
. We then can keep iterating this procedure. The bootstrap argument can be repeated until we reach the largest integer
k
such that
(1-k{\bar{\alpha}})>-m
: one last iteration of the argument gives then

|h(s)|\leq Ce^{-ms}

(5.42)

and hence, inserting one last time in (
5.40
)

|\Gamma(s)|\leq Ce^{-(m+{\bar{\alpha}})s}\,.

(5.43)

In order to estimate the first and second derivatives of
\Gamma
we observe that

\frac{I^{\prime}}{I}=-\alpha(im\beta\tilde{\zeta}+z_{0}-1+2\alpha)

and we compute explicitly

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\alpha(im\beta\tilde{\zeta}+z_{0}-1+2\alpha)\Gamma+imh\tilde{g}^{\prime}

(5.44)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\alpha im\beta\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}\Gamma+\alpha(im\beta\tilde{\zeta}+z_{0}-1+2\alpha)\Gamma^{\prime}+imh\tilde{g}^{\prime\prime}+imh^{\prime}\tilde{g}^{\prime}\,.

(5.45)

From (
5.44
) and the bounds (
5.43
),(
5.42
), and (
5.31
), we immediately conclude

|\Gamma^{\prime}(s)|\leq Ce^{-(m+{\bar{\alpha}})s}\,.

(5.46)

As for the second derivative, using (
5.46
), (
5.43
),(
5.42
), and (
5.31
), we conclude

|\alpha im\beta\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}\Gamma+\alpha(im\beta\tilde{\zeta}+z_{0}-1+2\alpha)\Gamma^{\prime}+imh\tilde{g}^{\prime\prime}|\leq Ce^{-(m+{\bar{\alpha}})s}\,.

In order to estimate the term
imh^{\prime}\tilde{g}^{\prime}
we differentiate (
5.39
) to infer

h^{\prime}(s)=\frac{1}{2}e^{-ms}\int_{-\infty}^{s}e^{ms^{\prime}}\Gamma(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}e^{ms}\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{-ms^{\prime}}\Gamma(s^{\prime})\,ds^{\prime}\,.

(5.47)

We then derive
|h^{\prime}(s)|\leq Ce^{-ms}
using the same argument for bounding
h
. In turn, combined again with (
5.31
) we conclude
|imh^{\prime}\tilde{g}^{\prime}(s)|\leq Ce^{-(m+{\bar{\alpha}})s}
, hence completing the proof of (
5.30
).

Step 4. Estimates at
-\infty
.

For the bound at
-\infty
we use instead (
5.32
) (which we observe holds for positive
s
as well). This leads to the inequality

|\Gamma(s)|\leq Ce^{(2+\alpha(a_{0}-1))s}\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha(a_{0}-1)\sigma}|h(\sigma)|\,d\sigma

(5.48)

In this argument we assume that
a_{0}
is selected very large, depending on
m
.
In turn we estimate
h
for negative
s
by

|h(s)|\leq Ce^{ms}+Ce^{ms}\int_{s}^{0}e^{-m\sigma}|\Gamma(\sigma)|\,d\sigma+Ce^{-ms}\int_{-\infty}^{s}e^{m\sigma}|\Gamma(\sigma)|\,d\sigma\,.

(5.49)

Observe now that we have
|h(s)|\leq Ce^{s}
for every
s
. Inserting this bound in (
5.48
) and assuming that
a_{0}
is large enough we conclude
|\Gamma(s)|\leq Ce^{3s}
. In turn we can insert the latter bound in (
5.49
) to conclude

|h(s)|\leq C(e^{ms}+e^{3s})
. Since
m\geq 2
we can then conclude
|h(s)|\leq Ce^{2s}
and inserting it in (
5.48
) we conclude
|\Gamma(s)|\leq Ce^{4s}
.

For the first and second derivatives we use the formulae (
5.44
), (
5.44
), and (
5.47
) and argue as above to conclude
|\Gamma^{\prime}(s)|+|\Gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)|\leq Ce^{4s}
.

Step 5. Estimate on
v=K_{2}*\eta
.
Recall that we can explicitly compute
K_{2}*\eta=\nabla^{\perp}\psi
and that we already argued that
v\in W^{1,2}_{loc}
. Hence to complete the proof we need to show

\int_{\{|x|\geq 1\}}|\nabla\psi|^{2}\,dx=\int_{1}^{\infty}\left((f^{\prime}(r))^{2}+\frac{(f(r))^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)\,r\,dr<\infty\,.

(5.50)

Recall next that
f(r)=h(\ln r)
and
f^{\prime}(r)=\frac{1}{r}h^{\prime}(\ln r)
. Thus the exponential decays for
h
proved in the previous steps imply
f(r)\leq C(1+r)^{-m}
and
f^{\prime}(r)\leq C(1+r)^{-m-1}
. Since
m\geq 2
, this clearly implies (
5.50
) and completes the proof.
∎

5.3.
Proof of the baseline
L^{2}
estimate

In this section we prove (
5.7
). In order to simplify the notation, from now on we will use
\Omega
in place
\Omega_{\text{per},k}
. We recall next equation (
2.47
)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\tau}-L_{\text{ss}})\Omega=

\displaystyle-\underbrace{(V_{\text{lin}}\cdot\nabla)\Omega}_{=:\mathscr{F}_{1}}-\underbrace{(V_{r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega}_{=:\mathscr{F}_{2}}-\underbrace{(V\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{\text{lin}}}_{=:\mathscr{F}_{3}}+\underbrace{(V\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{r}}_{=:\mathscr{F}_{4}}+\underbrace{(V\cdot\nabla)\Omega}_{=:\mathscr{F}_{5}}

\displaystyle-\underbrace{(V_{\text{lin}}\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{\text{lin}}}_{=:\mathscr{F}_{6}}-\underbrace{(V_{r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{\text{lin}}}_{=:\mathscr{F}_{7}}-\underbrace{(V_{\text{lin}}\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{r}}_{=:\mathscr{F}_{8}}\,.

(5.51)

We then define
\mathscr{F}:=-\sum_{i=1}^{8}\mathscr{F}_{i}
.
Recalling Theorem
3.0.4
and the fact that
\Omega(\cdot,-k)=0
, we estimate via Duhamel’s formula

\|\Omega(\cdot,\bar{\tau})\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(\varepsilon)\int_{-k}^{\bar{\tau}}e^{(a_{0}+\varepsilon)(\bar{\tau}-s)}\|\mathscr{F}(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{2}}\,ds\,.

(5.52)

We next estimate the
L^{2}
norms of the various
\mathscr{F}_{i}
. In order to keep our notation simpler we use
\|\cdot\|_{2}
for
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
for
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
\mathscr{F}_{1}
is simple:

\|\mathscr{F}_{1}(\cdot,s)\|_{2}\leq\|V_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,s)\|_{\infty}\|D\Omega(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{2}}\leq Ce^{a_{0}s}e^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})s}\leq Ce^{(2a_{0}+\delta_{0})s}\,.

(5.53)

As for
\mathscr{F}_{2}
we use the fact that

\displaystyle\int|\mathscr{F}_{2}(\xi,\tau)|^{2}\,d\xi

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}|\xi|^{2-{2{\bar{\alpha}}}}|D\Omega(\xi,\tau)|^{2}\,d\xi

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{{\frac{2{\bar{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\tau}\int|\xi|^{2}|D\Omega(\xi,\tau)|^{2}\,d\xi\leq Ce^{{\frac{2{\bar{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\tau}\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{X}\,.

We hence conclude

\|\mathscr{F}_{2}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+{\frac{{\bar{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}+\delta_{0})\tau}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+1+\delta_{0})\tau}\,.

(5.54)

As for
\mathscr{F}_{3}
, for every fixed
\tau
with
\kappa=\frac{1}{2}
we can use Proposition
5.0.2
to conclude

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{F}_{3}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Omega_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}}

(5.55)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{X}\|\nabla\Omega_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}}

(5.56)

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(2a_{0}+{\delta_{0}})\tau}\,.

(5.57)

To estimate
\mathscr{F}_{4}
we recall that

\Omega_{r}(\xi,\tau)=\beta(1-\chi(e^{\tau/\alpha}(\xi))\bar{\Omega}(\xi)+e^{\tau/\alpha}(\beta\zeta(|\xi|)|\xi|)\chi^{\prime}(e^{\tau/\alpha}\xi)\,.

(5.58)

Differentiating the latter identity we get:

\displaystyle|\nabla\Omega_{r}(\xi,\tau)|\leq

\displaystyle C\mathbf{1}_{|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}|D\bar{\Omega}|(\xi)

\displaystyle+Ce^{\tau/\alpha}(|\bar{\Omega}|(\xi)+|D(\zeta(|\xi|)|\xi|))\mathbf{1}_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R\geq|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}

\displaystyle+Ce^{2\tau/\alpha}(|\zeta(\xi)||\xi|)\mathbf{1}_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R\geq|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\mathbf{1}_{|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}|\xi|^{-1-{\bar{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle+C(e^{\tau/\alpha}|\xi|^{-{\bar{\alpha}}}+e^{2\tau/\alpha}|\xi|^{1-{\bar{\alpha}}})\mathbf{1}_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R\geq|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}

(5.59)

where we are assuming that
\textrm{spt}\,(\chi)\subset B_{R}
. We next use Proposition
5.0.2
with
\kappa=\alpha/2
to get
\|V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{X}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}
.
In particular we can estimate

\displaystyle\int|\mathscr{F}_{4}(\xi,\tau)|^{2}d\xi\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{2(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\int_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}}^{\infty}r^{-1-{2{\bar{\alpha}}}}dr

\displaystyle+Ce^{2(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/\alpha)\tau}\int_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}}^{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R}r^{1-{2{\bar{\alpha}}}}\,dr

\displaystyle+Ce^{2(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+2/\alpha)\tau}\int_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}}^{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R}r^{3-{2{\bar{\alpha}}}}\,dr\leq Ce^{(2a_{0}+2\delta_{0}+{2})\tau}\,.

We thus conclude

\|\mathscr{F}_{4}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1)\tau}\,.

(5.60)

For
\mathscr{F}_{5}
we use again
\|V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}
to get

\|\mathscr{F}_{5}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\|D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{2(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\,.

(5.61)

\mathscr{F}_{6}
follows easily from Lemma
5.0.3

\|\mathscr{F}_{6}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq\|\Omega_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}\|\Omega_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{2(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\,.

(5.62)

\mathscr{F}_{7}
and
\mathscr{F}_{8}
can be easily estimated using the explicit formula for
\Omega_{r}
and the decay estimates given by Lemma
5.0.3
for
\Omega_{\text{lin}}
, in particular they enjoy an estimate which is better than (
5.60
), i.e.

\|\mathscr{F}_{7}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}+\|\mathscr{F}_{8}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}\,.

(5.63)

Assuming that
a_{0}
is sufficiently large we then achieve the estimate

\|\mathscr{F}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}\,.

(5.64)

Inserting in (
5.52
) we choose
\varepsilon<1/2+\delta_{0}
and we then achieve

\|\Omega(\cdot,\bar{\tau})\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\varepsilon)\bar{\tau}}\int_{-k}^{\bar{\tau}}e^{(\delta_{0}+1/2-\varepsilon)s}\,ds\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\bar{\tau}}\,.

(5.65)

In fact, observe that the argument just given implies the stronger conclusion

\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}\,,\qquad\forall\tau\in[-k,\bar{\tau}]\,.

(5.66)

5.4.
Estimates on the first derivative

In this section we prove (
5.8
) and (
5.9
).
The proof will be achieved via
L^{2}
and
L^{4}
energy estimates, where we will differentiate (
2.47
) first with respect to the angular variable and then with respect to the radial variable. We start rewriting (
2.47
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Omega-\Omega+\left(\left(-\frac{\xi}{\alpha}+\beta\bar{V}+V_{r}+V_{\text{lin}}+V\right)\cdot\nabla\right)\Omega

\displaystyle=-\beta(V\cdot\nabla)\bar{\Omega}-(V\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{\text{lin}}-(V\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{r}-(V_{\text{lin}}\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{\text{lin}}

\displaystyle\qquad-(V_{r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{\text{lin}}-(V_{\text{lin}}\cdot\nabla)\Omega_{r}

\displaystyle=:\mathscr{G}\,.

(5.67)

We next differentiate in
\theta
. In order to simplify our notation we will write
\theta
in the subscript (or eventually
,\theta
if there is already another subscript). We also recall that
\Omega_{r}
,
\bar{\Omega}
are radial functions, while
(V_{r}\cdot\nabla)
and
(\bar{V}\cdot\nabla)
are angular derivatives times a function which is radial, and
\xi\cdot\nabla
is a radial derivative times a radial function. So we can write

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Omega_{\theta}-\Omega_{\theta}+\left(\left(-\frac{\xi}{\alpha}+\beta\bar{V}+V_{r}+V_{\text{lin}}+V\right)\cdot\nabla\right)\Omega_{\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{\theta}-(V_{\text{lin},\theta}\cdot\nabla)\Omega-(V_{\theta}\cdot\nabla)\Omega=:\mathscr{H}_{1}\,

(5.68)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\frac{\Omega_{\theta}}{r}-\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}+1\right)\frac{\Omega_{\theta}}{r}+\left(\left(-\frac{\xi}{\alpha}+\beta\bar{V}+V_{r}+V_{\text{lin}}+V\right)\cdot\nabla\right)\frac{\Omega_{\theta}}{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\mathscr{G}_{\theta}-\frac{1}{r}(V_{\text{lin},\theta}\cdot\nabla)\Omega-\frac{1}{r}(V_{\theta}\cdot\nabla)\Omega+\Omega_{\theta}((V_{\text{lin}}+V)\cdot\nabla)\frac{1}{r}=:\mathscr{H}_{2}\,.

(5.69)

We then multiply by
\Omega_{\theta}
the first equation and integrate by parts the terms on the left-hand side to conclude

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\frac{1}{2}\|\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)\|\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}^{2}+\int\mathscr{H}_{1}(\xi,\tau)\Omega_{\theta}(\xi,\tau)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathscr{H}_{1}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\|\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

(5.70)

Likewise we multiply the second identity by
(\frac{1}{r}\Omega_{\theta})^{3}
and integrate by parts to achieve

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\frac{1}{4}\|r^{-1}\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}^{4}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\right)\|r^{-1}\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}^{4}+\int\mathscr{H}_{2}(\xi,\tau)(r^{-1}\Omega_{\theta}(\xi,\tau))^{3}

which implies

\|r^{-1}\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq\int_{-k}^{\tau}e^{\left(1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\right)(\tau-\hat{\tau})}\|\mathscr{H}_{2}(\cdot,\hat{\tau})\|_{4}\,d\hat{\tau}\,\quad\forall\tau\in[-k,\bar{\tau}]\,.

(5.71)

We next wish to estimate the two integrals in the right-hand sides of both equations.

We summarize the relevant estimates in the Lemma
5.4.1
below. Note that they imply

\displaystyle\|\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\hat{\tau})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\hat{\tau}}\,\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\hat{\tau}\in[-k,\bar{\tau}]\,,

(5.72)

\displaystyle\|r^{-1}\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\hat{\tau})\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\hat{\tau}}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\hat{\tau}\in[-k,\bar{\tau}]\,,

(5.73)

provided
a_{0}
is chosen big enough.

Lemma 5.4.1
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
5.0.4
we have

\displaystyle\|D\mathscr{G}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}

(5.74)

\displaystyle\|rD\mathscr{G}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}

(5.75)

\displaystyle\||DV_{\text{lin}}||\nabla\Omega|(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}+\||DV||\nabla\Omega|(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}

(5.76)

\displaystyle\|r|DV_{\text{lin}}||\nabla\Omega|(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}+\|r|DV||\nabla\Omega|(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}

(5.77)

\displaystyle\|r^{-1}V_{\text{lin}}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}+\|r^{-1}VD\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}\,.

(5.78)

Proof.

Proof of (
5.74
) and of (
5.75
).
We break the terms as

\displaystyle\|D\mathscr{G}\|_{4}\leq

\displaystyle C\|DVD\bar{\Omega}\|_{4}+C\|VD^{2}\bar{\Omega}\|_{4}+\|DVD\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}

\displaystyle+\|VD^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}+C\|DVD\Omega_{r}\|_{4}+C\|VD^{2}\Omega_{r}\|_{4}

\displaystyle+C\|DV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}+C\|V_{\text{lin}}D^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}+\|DV_{r}D\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}

\displaystyle+\|V_{r}D^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}+\|DV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega_{r}\|_{4}+\|V_{\text{lin}}D^{2}\Omega_{r}\|_{4}\,,

(5.79)

and

\displaystyle\|rD\mathscr{G}\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle C\|rDVD\bar{\Omega}\|_{2}+C\|rVD^{2}\bar{\Omega}\|_{2}+\|rDVD\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\|rVD^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{2}+C\|rDVD\Omega_{r}\|_{2}+C\|VrD^{2}\Omega_{r}\|_{2}

\displaystyle+C\|rDV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{2}+C\|rV_{\text{lin}}D^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{2}+\|rDV_{r}D\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\|rV_{r}D^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{2}+\|rDV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega_{r}\|_{2}+\|rV_{\text{lin}}D^{2}\Omega_{r}\|_{2}\,.

(5.80)

The terms involving
\Omega
and
\bar{\Omega}
is where we use the baseline
L^{2}
estimate. Observe that

\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}^{1/2}\|D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|^{1/2}_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/4)\tau}\,

(5.81)

and, by Calderón-Zygmund,

\|DK_{2}*\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq C\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/4)\tau}\,.

(5.82)

Next we estimate

\displaystyle\|DVD\bar{\Omega}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq\|D\bar{\Omega}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}\|DV(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/4)\tau}\,,

(5.83)

\displaystyle\|rDVD\bar{\Omega}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|rD\bar{\Omega}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}\|DV(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\,\tau}\,.

(5.84)

Next, recalling Lemma
2.4.1
we get

\|V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\leq CR\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq CRe^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}\,.

(5.85)

However, using
\int_{B_{R}}V(\cdot,\tau)=0
, we can in fact estimate also

\|V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{4}(B_{R})}\leq CR^{1/2}\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq CR^{1/2}e^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}\,.

(5.86)

In particular we can infer

\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-(1+\varepsilon)}V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq C(\varepsilon)e^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}

(5.87)

and

\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-(1/2+\varepsilon)}V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq C(\varepsilon)e^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}

(5.88)

for every positive
\varepsilon
. On the other hand, given that
|D^{2}\bar{\Omega}(\xi)|\leq C(1+|\xi|)^{-2-{\bar{\alpha}}}
, we easily infer

\displaystyle\|VD^{2}\bar{\Omega}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-{1}}V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}

(5.89)

\displaystyle\|rVD^{2}\bar{\Omega}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-1-{\bar{\alpha}}}V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}\,.

(5.90)

From now on we will not handle the terms with the weight
r
as the proof is entirely analogous: we will just focus on the
L^{4}
estimates and leave to the reader the computations with the weight.

For the two quadratic terms in
V_{\text{lin}}
we can use Lemma
5.0.3
to achieve

\|DV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}+\|V_{\text{lin}}D^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{2a_{0}\tau}\,.

(5.91)

Likewise we can estimate

\|DVD\Omega_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}+\|VD^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}\leq Ce^{a_{0}\tau}\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{X}\leq Ce^{(2a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\,,

(5.92)

(where for the second term we use the decay at infinity of
D^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}
to compensate the moderate growth of
V
, the argument is the same as for (
5.89
) and we do not repeat it here).
Observe next that, by (
5.59
),
\|D\Omega_{r}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}\leq C
for
\tau\leq 0
. Hence the term
DVD\Omega_{r}
can be estimated as in (
5.83
):

\|DVD\Omega_{r}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq C\|DV(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/4)\tau}\,.

(5.93)

As for the other term, differentiating once more and arguing as for (
5.59
) we get:

\displaystyle|D^{2}\Omega_{r}(\xi,\tau)|\leq

\displaystyle C|\xi|^{-2-{\bar{\alpha}}}\mathbf{1}_{|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}

\displaystyle+(e^{\tau/\alpha}|\xi|^{-1-{\bar{\alpha}}}+e^{2\tau/\alpha}|\xi|^{-{\bar{\alpha}}}+e^{3\tau/\alpha}|\xi|^{1-{\bar{\alpha}}})\mathbf{1}_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R\geq|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C|\xi|^{-2-{\bar{\alpha}}}\mathbf{1}_{|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}+Ce^{3\tau/\alpha}|\xi|^{1-{\bar{\alpha}}}\mathbf{1}_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R\geq|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}\,.

(5.94)

We can thus argue similarly as for (
5.89
) to conclude

\displaystyle\|VD^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{r}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-3/2}V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}+Ce^{3\tau/\alpha}\|V\|_{L^{4}(B_{Re^{-\xi/\tau}})}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/4)\tau}\,.

(5.95)

In order to handle the remaining three terms, we recall that, by Lemma
5.0.3
,

\displaystyle\|V_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{a_{0}\tau}

(5.96)

\displaystyle|DV_{\text{lin}}(\xi,\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{a_{0}\tau}|\xi|^{-2-{\bar{\alpha}}}

(5.97)

\displaystyle|D^{k}\Omega_{\text{lin}}(\xi,\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{a_{0}\tau}|\xi|^{-2-k-{\bar{\alpha}}}\,.

(5.98)

On the other hand, owing to the computations in this and the previous section we can also write

\displaystyle|V_{r}(\xi,\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C|\xi|^{1-{\bar{\alpha}}}\mathbf{1}_{|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}

\displaystyle|DV_{r}(\xi,\tau)|+|\Omega_{r}(\xi,\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C|\xi|^{-{\bar{\alpha}}}\mathbf{1}_{|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}+Ce^{\tau/\alpha}|\xi|^{1-{\bar{\alpha}}}\mathbf{1}_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R\geq|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}

\displaystyle|D^{k}\Omega_{r}(\xi,\tau)|
