\displaystyle\leq C|\xi|^{-k-{\bar{\alpha}}}\mathbf{1}_{|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}

\displaystyle\qquad+Ce^{k\tau/\alpha}|\xi|^{1-{\bar{\alpha}}}\mathbf{1}_{e^{-\tau/\alpha}R\geq|\xi|\geq e^{-\tau/\alpha}}\,.

Integrating the estimates in the respective domain we easily get

\|DV_{r}D\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}+\|V_{r}D^{2}\Omega_{\text{lin}}\|_{4}+\|DV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega_{r}\|_{4}+\|V_{\text{lin}}D^{2}\Omega_{r}\|_{4}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+1)\tau}\,.

(5.99)

Remaining estimates.
The two terms (
5.76
) and (
5.77
) have already been covered in the argument above. It remains to handle (
5.78
). Notice that, by Lemma
5.0.3
and Proposition
5.0.2
we have

\displaystyle\|r^{-1}V(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{X}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}

(5.100)

\displaystyle\|r^{-1}V_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{a_{0}\tau}\,.

(5.101)

We thus conclude easily

\displaystyle\|r^{-1}VD\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\|D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{2(a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\,

(5.102)

\displaystyle\|r^{-1}V_{\text{lin}}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{a_{0}\tau}\|D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{(2a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\,.

(5.103)

∎

We next differentiate in
r
(
5.67
) in order to achieve similar identities to (
5.68
) and (
5.69
). This time, given the ambiguity with
V_{r}
and
\Omega_{r}
, we write
,r
in the subscript to denote the radial derivative of
any
function.

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Omega_{,r}+\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)\Omega_{,r}+\left(\left(-\frac{\xi}{\alpha}+\beta\bar{V}+V_{r}+V_{\text{lin}}+V\right)\cdot\nabla\right)\Omega_{,r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{,r}-(V_{\text{lin},r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega-(V_{,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega-\beta(\bar{V}_{,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega-(V_{r,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega

(5.104)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}r\Omega_{,r}+r\Omega_{,r}+\left(\left(-\frac{\xi}{\alpha}+\beta\bar{V}+V_{r}+V_{\text{lin}}+V\right)\cdot\nabla\right)(r\Omega_{,r})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\mathscr{G}_{,r}-r(V_{\text{lin},r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega-r(V_{,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega-r\beta(\bar{V}_{,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega-r(V_{r,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega

\displaystyle+\Omega_{,r}(V_{\text{lin}}+V)\cdot\nabla r\,.

(5.105)

Multiplying by
(\Omega_{,r})^{3}
and
r\Omega_{,r}
respectively, and using the estimates (
5.74
) and (
5.75
), we achieve, in the respective cases:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\|\Omega_{,r}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}+C\|DV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}+C\|DVD\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+C\|\bar{V}_{,r}\cdot\nabla\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}+\|DV_{r}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\,,

(5.106)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\|r\Omega_{,r}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}+C\|rV_{\text{lin},r}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle+C\|r(V_{,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}+C\|r\bar{V}_{,r}\cdot\nabla\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\|r(V_{r,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}+\|V_{\text{lin}}D\Omega\|_{2}+\|VD\Omega\|_{2}\,.

(5.107)

Note next that

\displaystyle\|DV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}+\|DVD\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

(5.108)

\displaystyle\leq(\|DV_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}+\|DV(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty})\|D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{(2a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\,,

and likewise

\displaystyle\|rDV_{\text{lin}}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\|rDVD\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\|DV_{\text{lin}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty}+\|DV(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\infty})\|rD\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(2a_{0}+\delta_{0})\tau}\,,

(5.109)

The terms
(\bar{V}_{,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega
and
r(\bar{V}_{,r}\cdot\nabla)\Omega
can be bounded observing that they involve only the angular derivative and that
\bar{V}_{,r}
is bounded. Since the angular derivative has already been estimated (this is the reason for estimating it
before
estimating the radial derivative), we get

\displaystyle\|\bar{V}_{,r}\cdot\nabla\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\|r^{-1}\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}\,.

(5.110)

\displaystyle\|r\bar{V}_{,r}\cdot\nabla\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Omega_{\theta}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+2\delta_{0})\tau}\,.

(5.111)

As for
DV_{r}D\Omega
and
rDV_{r}D\Omega
we observe that
\|DV_{r}\|_{\infty}\leq e^{\tau}
and thus
we easily get

\displaystyle\|DV_{r}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{\tau}\|D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1)\tau}

(5.112)

\displaystyle\|rDV_{r}D\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{\tau}\|rD\Omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1)\tau}\,.

(5.113)

We finally need to estimate
\|V_{\text{lin}}D\Omega\|_{2}
and
\|VD\Omega\|_{2}
, but we observe that this has already been done in the previous section, since they correspond to the terms
\mathscr{F}_{1}
and
\mathscr{F}_{4}
in (
5.51
), cf. (
5.53
) and (
5.60
).
Summarizing, we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\|\Omega_{,r}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}

(5.114)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\|r\Omega_{,r}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(a_{0}+\delta_{0}+1/2)\tau}\,,

(5.115)

which we then integrate between
0
and
\bar{\tau}
to achieve the desired conclusion.

Appendix A
A more detailed spectral analysis

A.1.
From Remark
4.0.3
(i) to Remark
3.0.2
(c)

Let us assume the validity of
4.0.3
(i) and prove Remark
3.0.2
(c). Let
m_{0}\geq 2
be the integer such that

\displaystyle\textrm{spec}\,(L_{\text{st}},U_{m_{0}})\cap\{\textrm{Re}\,z>0\}\neq\emptyset\,,

(A.1)

\displaystyle\textrm{spec}\,(L_{\text{st}},U_{m})\cap\{\textrm{Re}\,z>0\}=\emptyset\,\quad\text{for any $m>m_{0}$}\,.

(A.2)

We show that Remark
3.0.2
(c) holds with
m=m_{0}
.

For any
z\in\textrm{spec}_{m_{0}}\,(L_{\text{st}})\cap\{\textrm{Re}\,z>0\}
we denote by
V_{z}:=P_{z}(L^{2}_{m_{0}})
the image of the Riesz projector

P_{z}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma}(w-L_{\text{st}})^{-1}dw\,,

where
\gamma
parameterizes the boundary of a ball containing
z
and no other eigenvalues of
L_{\text{st}}
.

It is enough to show that
P_{z}(U_{km_{0}})=\{0\}
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{-1,1\}
,
z\in\textrm{spec}_{m_{0}}\,(L_{\text{st}})\cap\{\textrm{Re}\,z>0\}
since it gives

V_{z}=P_{z}(U_{m_{0}}\cup U_{-m_{0}})\subset U_{m_{0}}\cup U_{-m_{0}}\,,

where the second inclusion follows from the fact that
U_{m}
is always an invariant space of
L_{\text{s}t}
.

If
k>1
, from (
A.1
) we know that
z\notin\textrm{spec}\,(L_{\text{st}},U_{km_{0}})
, hence
P_{z}(U_{km_{0}})
is trivial. If
k<-1
,
we reduce to the previous situation by observing that
P_{z}(U_{km_{0}})=\overline{P_{\bar{z}}(U_{-km_{0}})}
.

A.2.
Proof of Remark
4.0.3
(i)

In order to show this point, given Lemma
4.9.1
, we just need to prove the following statement.

Lemma A.2.1
.

For every fixed
\Xi\in\mathscr{C}
there is
M_{0}>0
such that
\mathscr{U}_{m}
is empty for every
m\geq M_{0}
.

Indeed, given the conclusion above we infer that
\mathscr{U}_{m}
is empty for every
m\geq m_{a}
and it thus suffices to select
m_{0}
as the largest integer strictly smaller than
m_{a}
.

Before coming to the proof of Lemma
A.2.1
we state an auxiliary fact which will be used in the argument and which can be readily inferred from the computations in Step 1 of the proof of Lemma
4.8.1
.

Lemma A.2.2
.

For every
0<\sigma<\tau<1
there is a constant
C
(depending only upon
\sigma
and
\tau
) such that
B_{z}:=\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\circ\frac{1}{\Xi-z}
is a bounded operator from
C^{\sigma}
to
C^{\tau}
for every
z
with
\textrm{Im}\,z>0
and

\|B_{z}\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\sigma},C^{\tau})}\leq C\,.

Proof of Lemma
A.2.1
.

The proof will be by contradiction and thus, assuming that the statement is false, we can select:

(i)

a sequence
\{m_{j}\}\subset[1,\infty[
with
m_{j}\to\infty
;

(ii)

a sequence
\{z_{j}\}\subset\mathbb{C}
with
\textrm{Im}\,z_{j}>0
;

(iii)

and a sequence
\{\psi_{j}\}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
solving the equation

-\frac{d^{2}\psi_{j}}{dt^{2}}+m_{j}^{2}\psi_{j}+\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\psi_{j}=0\,.

(A.3)

Step 1.
We first prove that
\{z_{j}\}
is bounded and every cluster point must be an element of
[0,\Xi(-\infty)]
. Otherwise for a subsequence, not relabeled, we get the estimate

\sup\left\|\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}=:C_{0}<\infty\,.

By scalar multiplying (
A.3
) by
\psi_{j}
and taking the real part of the resulting equation we then conclude

\int(|\psi_{j}^{\prime}|^{2}+m_{j}^{2}|\psi_{j}|^{2})\leq C_{0}\int|\psi_{j}|^{2}\,,

which clearly it is not feasible because
C_{0}<m_{j}^{2}
for a sufficiently large
j
(and
\psi_{j}
is nontrivial).

Up to subsequences we can thus assume that
z_{j}
converges to some
z_{0}\in[0,\Xi(-\infty)]
.

Step 2.
We next analyze the cases
z_{0}=0
and
z_{0}=\Xi(-\infty)
. The argument is similar to that used in Section
4.6
in case (C). Let us argue first for
z_{0}=0
. We observe that
\Xi^{-1}|A|
belongs to
L^{1}(]-\infty,N])
for any fixed
N
and that, likewise,
|\Xi-z_{j}|^{-1}|A|
have a uniform
L^{1}
bound on any
]-\infty,N]
. We can then
use the Lemma
4.5.2
to normalize
\psi_{j}
so that it is asymptotic to
e^{m_{j}t}
and also to write

\psi_{j}(t)=e^{m_{j}t}(1+z_{j}(t))

with

|z_{j}(t)|\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{m_{j}}\int_{-\infty}^{N}\frac{|A|}{|\Xi-z_{j}|}\right)-1\qquad\mbox{for all $t\leq N$.}

In particular, we have
|z_{j}(t)|\leq\frac{C(N)}{m_{j}}
on
]-\infty,N]
. We next scalar multiply (
A.3
) by
\psi_{j}
and take the imaginary part to conclude

-\left(\int_{-\infty}^{a}+\int_{b}^{\infty}\right)\frac{A}{|\Xi-z_{j}|^{2}}|\psi_{j}|^{2}\leq\int_{a}^{b}\frac{A}{|\Xi-z_{j}|^{2}}|\psi_{j}|^{2}\,.

In particular, since
\frac{A}{|\Xi-z_{j}|^{2}}
is bounded from above by a constant
C
independent of
j
on
[a,b]
and
-\frac{A}{|\Xi-z_{j}|^{2}}
is bounded from below by a constant
c>0
independent of
j
on
[b+1,b+2]
, we conclude

\int_{b+1}^{b+2}|\psi_{j}|^{2}\leq\frac{C}{c}\int_{a}^{b}|\psi_{j}|^{2}\,.

We next choose
N
larger than
b+2
and use the estimate
|z_{j}(t)|\leq\frac{C(N)}{m_{j}}
to argue that, for
j
large enough, we have
\frac{1}{2}e^{m_{j}t}\leq|\psi_{j}(t)|\leq 2e^{m_{j}t}
on
]-\infty,N]
. In particular, we infer

\int_{b+1}^{b+2}e^{2m_{j}t}\leq C\int_{a}^{b}e^{2m_{j}t}

provided the constant
C
is chosen large enough (but independent of
j
) and
j
is large enough. The latter inequality is certainly impossible for
m_{j}
large enough, leading to a contradiction.

The argument to exclude
z_{0}=\Xi(-\infty)
is entirely analogous, this time normalizing for
t\to\infty
and reaching an inequality of type

\int_{a-2}^{a-1}e^{-2m_{j}t}\leq C\int_{a}^{b}e^{-2m_{j}t}

for a constant
C
independent of
j
and any
j
large enough.

Step 3.
We next examine the last case, that is
z_{0}=\Xi(c)
. This time we fix a
\sigma\in\,]0,1[
and normalize
\psi_{j}
so that

\|\psi_{j}\|_{C^{\sigma}}=1\,.

(A.4)

We observe that

\psi_{j}=-\mathcal{K}_{m_{j}}\left(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\psi_{j}\right)\,,

and also recall that
\mathcal{K}_{m_{j}}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{2m_{j}}e^{-m_{j}|\cdot|}*\varphi
.
We set
m_{0}=m_{a}
write further

\psi_{j}=-\mathcal{K}_{m_{j}}\circ\left(-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\right)\left(\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\left(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\right)\psi_{j}\right)\,.

Recalling Lemma
A.2.2
, we can fix a
\tau\in]\sigma,1[
to achieve

\left\|\left(\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\left(\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{j}}\right)\psi_{j}\right)\right\|_{C^{\tau}}\leq C

for some constant
C
independent of
j
.

We will show in the final step that

(Cl)

\|\mathcal{K}_{m_{j}}\circ(-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2})\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\tau},C^{\tau})}\leq C
for some constant
C
independent of
k
.

In particular, we achieve

\|\psi_{j}\|_{C^{\tau}}\leq C\,.

(A.5)

We now wish to show that indeed
\|\psi_{j}\|_{C^{\sigma}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
for
j
large enough, which obviously would be a contradiction. In order to achieve the latter estimate we use a Littlewood-Paley decomposition. We fix a cut-off function
\chi
which is supported in
]\frac{1}{2},2[
, define
\chi_{\ell}(t):=\chi(2^{-\ell}t)
for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and assume that
\chi
has been chosen so that

\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}\chi_{\ell}\equiv 1\qquad\mbox{on $[1,\infty[$}.

We then define

\chi_{-1}:=1-\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\chi_{\ell}\,

and introduce the Littlewood-Paley operator
\Delta_{\ell}
as
\Delta_{\ell}(\varphi)=\mathscr{F}^{-1}(\chi_{\ell}\mathscr{F}(\varphi))
, where
\mathscr{F}
is the Fourier transform.
We finally recall that (see
[
18
, Section 1.4.2]
), if we define

\|\varphi\|_{X^{\sigma}}:=\sum_{\ell\geq-1}2^{\sigma\ell}\|\Delta_{\ell}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}\,,

then

C(\sigma)^{-1}\|\varphi\|_{X^{\sigma}}\leq\|\varphi\|_{C^{\sigma}}\leq C(\sigma)\|\varphi\|_{X^{\sigma}}\,.

We are now ready to perform our final estimate. We fix a large
N
, which will be chosen later, and for
\ell\geq N
we write

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\geq N}2^{\sigma\ell}\|\Delta_{\ell}\psi_{j}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-N(\tau-\sigma)}\sum_{\ell\geq N}2^{\tau\ell}\|\Delta_{\ell}\psi_{j}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-N(\tau-\sigma)}C(\tau)\|\psi_{j}\|_{C^{\tau}}\leq C2^{-N(\tau-\sigma)}\,,

where the constant
C
is independent of both
N
and
j
. Next, for any
\ell
we observe that

\Delta_{\ell}\psi_{j}=\mathcal{K}_{m_{j}}\circ\left(-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\right)\underbrace{\left(\Delta_{\ell}\left(\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\left(\frac{A}{\Xi-z}\psi_{j}\right)\right)\right)}_{=:\Gamma_{\ell,j}}\,.

Now

\|\Gamma_{\ell,j}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C2^{-\ell\sigma}\left\|\mathcal{K}_{m_{0}}\left(\frac{A}{\Xi-z}\psi_{j}\right)\right\|_{C^{\sigma}}\leq C2^{-\ell\sigma}\,.

On the other hand, because of the frequency localization, we have

\Delta_{\ell}\psi_{j}=\mathcal{K}_{m_{j}}\circ\left(-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\right)\circ(\Delta_{\ell-1}+\Delta_{\ell}+\Delta_{\ell+1})(\Gamma_{\ell,j})

and the estimate

\left\|\mathcal{K}_{m_{j}}\circ\left(-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\right)\circ\Delta_{\ell}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{\infty},L^{\infty})}\leq\frac{C}{m_{j}^{2}}\left(2^{2\ell}+m_{0}^{2}\right)\,.

We can therefore write the estimate

\displaystyle\|\psi_{j}\|_{C^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{m_{j}^{2}}\sum_{\ell=-1}^{N}(2^{2\ell}+m_{0}^{2})+C2^{-N(\tau-\sigma)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{CN}{m_{j}^{2}}\left(2^{2N}+m_{0}^{2}\right)+C2^{-N(\tau-\sigma)}\,,

where the constants
C
are independent of
N
and
j
. In particular, we fix first
N
large enough to get
C2^{-N(\tau-\sigma)}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and we then choose
m_{j}
large enough so that

\frac{CN}{m_{j}^{2}}\left(2^{2N}+m_{0}^{2}\right)\leq\frac{1}{4}\,.

These two estimates imply
\|\psi_{j}\|_{C^{\sigma}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, contradicting the normalization (
A.4
).

Step 4.
To complete the proof of the Lemma we need to show (Cl). We first write

T_{m,\ell}:=\Delta_{\ell}\circ\mathcal{K}_{m}\circ\left(-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\right)\,.

The operator
T_{m,\ell}
is the convolution with a kernel
K_{m,\ell}
whose Fourier symbol is given by

\chi_{\ell}(\xi)\frac{|\xi|^{2}+m_{0}^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}+m^{2}}
. Hence, for
\ell\geq 0
we have

K_{m,\ell}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\chi\left(\frac{\xi}{2^{\ell}}\right)\frac{|\xi|^{2}+m_{0}^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}+m^{2}}e^{i\xi t}\,d\xi\,.

and

(-it)^{k}K_{m,\ell}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\frac{d^{k}}{d\xi^{k}}\left(\chi\left(\frac{\xi}{2^{\ell}}\right)\frac{|\xi|^{2}+m_{0}^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}+m^{2}}\right)e^{it\xi}\,d\xi\,.

In particular, we easily conclude

\||t|^{k}K_{m,\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C(k)2^{\ell(1-k)}\,,

for a constant
C(k)
independent of both
m\geq 1
and
\ell
, but which depends on
k
. From the latter we can estimate

\displaystyle\|K_{m,\ell}\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|t|\leq 2^{-\ell}}|K_{m,\ell}(s)|\,ds+\int_{|t|\geq 2^{-\ell}}\frac{|s^{2}K_{m,\ell}(s)|}{|s|^{2}}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq C+C2^{-\ell}\int_{2^{-\ell}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{s^{2}}\,ds\leq C\,.

For
\ell=-1
we just conclude, likewise

\||t|^{k}K_{m,-1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C(k)

for a constant
C(k)
independent of
m
, but depending of
k
. Once again using the cases
k=0
and
2
of the latter inequality we achieve

\|K_{m,-1}\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\,.

We have thus bounded all
\|K_{m,\ell}\|_{L^{1}}
with a universal constant
C
independent of both
m\geq 1
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}
. In particular, since
\|T_{m,\ell}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{\infty},L^{\infty})}=\|K_{m,\ell}\|_{L^{1}}
and

\mathcal{K}_{m}\circ\left(-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\right)=\sum_{\ell\geq-1}T_{m,\ell}=\sum_{\ell\geq-1}T_{m,\ell}\circ(\Delta_{\ell-1}+\Delta_{\ell}+\Delta_{\ell+1})\,,

we can estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{K}_{m}\circ\left(-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m_{0}^{2}\right)(\varphi)\right\|_{C^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\sigma)\sum_{\ell\geq-1}2^{\sigma\ell}\|T_{m,\ell}(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=C(\sigma)\sum_{\ell\geq-1}2^{\sigma\ell}\|T_{m,\ell}(\Delta_{\ell}\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\sigma)\sum_{\ell\geq-1}2^{\sigma\ell}\|\Delta_{\ell}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\sigma)\|\varphi\|_{C^{\sigma}}\,.

This completes the proof of (Cl) and hence of the entire Lemma.
∎

A.3.
Proof of Theorem
4.0.4

In
[
40
]
, Vishik claims the following improved version of Theorem
4.2.4
, which would immediately imply Theorem
4.0.4
.

Theorem A.3.1
.

There are a function
\Xi\in\mathscr{C}
and an integer
m_{0}\geq 2
such that
\mathscr{U}_{m}=\emptyset
for any integer
m>m_{0}
and
\mathscr{U}_{m_{0}}
consists of a single
z
. Moreover, the algebraic multiplicity of
m_{0}z
as an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{m_{0}}
is
1
.

Vishik’s suggested proof of Theorem
A.3.1
builds upon Proposition
4.4.1
and the following improved versions of Proposition
4.4.3
and Proposition
4.4.5
.

Proposition A.3.2
.

Assume
-\lambda_{a}<-1
and let
m_{a}=\sqrt{\lambda_{a}}
. Then there
exist
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta>0
with the following property.
For every
h\in]0,\delta[
,
\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}-h}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(\Xi(a))=\{z_{m_{a}-h}\}
, where
(m_{a}-h)z_{m_{a}-h}
is an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}
with algebraic multiplicity
1
.

In
[
40
]
Vishik only gives the argument that
\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}-h}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(\Xi(a))
contains a single element
z_{m_{a}-h}
and the corresponding eigenspace of
(m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}
has dimension
1
(i.e. its
geometric
multiplicity is
1
, cf. Remark
4.4.4
). However it is essential to have the
algebraic
multiplicity equal to
1
in order to complete his suggested argument. After we pointed out to him the gap in his paper, he suggested in
[
41
]
the proof of Proposition
A.3.2
reported below. Before coming to it, we point out that a spectral perturbation argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.9.1
(which we outline anyway below)
easily implies the following.

Proposition A.3.3
.

Assume
-\lambda_{a}<-1
and let

\displaystyle m_{a}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\lambda_{a}}\,,

\displaystyle m_{b}

\displaystyle:=\max\{1,\sqrt{\lambda_{b}}\}\,.

Then
\mathscr{U}_{m}
consists of a single element
z_{m}
for every
m\in]m_{b},m_{a}[
and moreover the algebraic multiplicity of
z_{m}
as an eigenvalue of
m^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m}
is
1
.

Taking the previous proposition for granted, we just need a choice of
\Xi
for which
\lambda_{a}>1
and
]m_{b},m_{a}[
contains an integer, which is guaranteed by Lemma
4.4.7
, and we conclude Theorem
A.3.1
.

We now explain how to prove Proposition
A.3.3
.
From Proposition
4.4.5
and Lemma
A.2.1
we know that
\mathscr{U}_{m}\neq\emptyset
, for every
m\in]m_{b},m_{a}[
, and
\mathscr{U}_{m}=\emptyset
for
m\geq m_{a}
. Moreover, Remark
4.9.2
implies that the sum of the algebraic multiplicities of
z\in\mathscr{U}_{m}
, as eigenvalues of
m^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m}
, is constant for
m\in]m_{b},m_{a}[
. Hence, to conclude we just need to prove that the latter is
1
for some
m\in]m_{b},m_{a}[
.
To that aim we show that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that
\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}-h}=\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}-h}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(\Xi(a))
for any
h\in]0,\delta[
. This is enough for our purposes since, together with Proposition
A.3.2
, it gives
\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}-h}=\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}-h}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(\Xi(a))=\{z_{m_{a}-h}\}
where
z_{m_{a}-h}
is an eigenvalue of
(m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}
with algebraic multiplicity
1
.

Assume for contradiction the existence of a sequence
(m_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
in
]m_{b},m_{a}[
converging to
m_{a}
such that there are
z_{j}\in\mathscr{U}_{m_{j}}
with
|z_{j}-\Xi(a)|>\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Up to extracting a subsequence, we may assume
z_{j}\to z
for some
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|z-\Xi(a)|\geq\varepsilon
. Proposition
4.4.1
implies that the imaginary part of
z
is positive. Arguing as in the first step of proof of Proposition
4.4.1
we can prove that
z\in\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}}
and reach a contradiction.

A.4.
Proof of Proposition
A.3.2

The proof of Proposition
A.3.2
can be reduced to the following weaker version using Remark
4.9.2
and the argument outlined in the previous paragraph.

Proposition A.4.1
.

Assume
-\lambda_{a}<-1
and let
m_{a}=\sqrt{\lambda_{a}}
. Let
h
and
\varepsilon
be sufficiently small so that Proposition
4.4.1
and Remark
4.4.4
apply, namely

\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}-h}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(\Xi(a))=\{z_{m_{a}-h}\}
, where
z_{m_{a}-h}
is an eigenvalue of
(m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}
with geometric multiplicity
1
. Then, if
h
is chosen possibly smaller, the algebraic multiplicity of
z_{m_{a}-h}
is also
1
.

We now come to the proof of the latter, which is the heart of the matter. First of all, we introduce a suitable transformation of the space
\mathcal{H}
(which we recall is the domain of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{m}
, defined in (
4.1
)). We introduce the Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}^{e}:=\left\{f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}\,:\,\int|f(t)|^{2}e^{-2t}\,dt<\infty\right\}

and the isometry
T:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}^{e}
given by

\gamma(r)\mapsto e^{2t}\gamma(e^{t})\,.

Rather than considering the operator
\mathcal{L}_{m}
on
\mathcal{H}
, it turns out to be more convenient to consider the operator
T\circ\mathcal{L}_{m}\circ T^{-1}
on
\mathcal{H}^{e}
. Since the spectra of the two operators coincide, with a slight abuse of notation we will keep writing
\mathcal{L}_{m}
in place of
T\circ\mathcal{L}_{m}\circ T^{-1}
, and we will keep
\mathscr{U}_{m}
to denote the point spectrum of
{m^{-1}}T\circ\mathcal{L}_{m}\circ T^{-1}
in the upper half plane.

Simple computations show that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{m}
is given, on
\mathcal{H}^{e}
by

\mathcal{L}_{m}(\alpha)=m\Xi\alpha-mA\varphi

where
\varphi
is the unique
\mathcal{H}^{e}
solution of

\varphi^{\prime\prime}-m^{2}\varphi=\alpha\,.

We can now come to the main idea behind the simplicity of
z_{m_{a}-h}
, which is borrowed from
[
41
]
. A prominent role is played by the adjoint of
\mathcal{L}_{m}
(considered as a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{H}^{e}
into itself): for the latter we will use the notation
\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\star}
.

Lemma A.4.2
.

Suppose that
h
and
\varepsilon
are sufficiently small such that

\{z_{m_{a}-h}\}=\mathscr{U}_{m_{a}-h}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(\Xi(a))

and
z_{m_{a}-h}
has geometric multiplicity
1
in
\textrm{spec}\,((m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h},\mathcal{H}^{e})
. Let
\alpha_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{e}\setminus\{0\}
be such that
(m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}(\alpha_{h})-z_{m_{a}-h}\alpha_{h}=0
. If
h
is small enough, then there is
\beta_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{e}
such that
(m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}^{\star}(\beta_{h})-\bar{z}_{m_{a}-h}\beta_{h}=0
and

\langle\alpha_{h},\beta_{h}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{e}}=\int\alpha_{h}(t)\bar{\beta}_{h}(t)\,e^{-2t}dt\neq 0\,.

(A.6)

Let us show how the latter implies Proposition
A.4.1
. Assume
z_{m_{a}-h}
were an element of
\textrm{spec}\,((m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h},\mathcal{H}^{e})\cap B_{\varepsilon}(\Xi(a))
with geometric multiplicity
1
and algebraic multiplicity larger than
1
: our goal is to show that
h
cannot be too small.
The properties just listed mean that the following bounded operator on
\mathcal{H}^{e}
,

L_{h}:=(m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}-z_{m_{a}-h}\,,

has a 1-dimensional kernel,
0
is in its point spectrum, and
0
has algebraic multiplicity strictly larger than
1
. These properties imply that any element
\alpha_{h}
in the kernel of
L_{h}
(i.e. any eigenfunction of
(m_{a}-h)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}
with eigenvalue
z_{m_{a}-h}
) is in the image of
L_{h}
. Fix one such element
\alpha_{h}
and let
\eta_{h}
be such that
L_{h}(\eta_{h})=\alpha_{h}
. If
h
is small enough, we can fix
\beta_{h}
as in Lemma
A.4.2
, and observe that it is in the kernel of the adjoint operator
L^{\star}_{h}
. We then must have

0\neq\int\alpha_{h}\bar{\beta}_{h}\,e^{-2t}dt=\int L_{h}(\eta_{h})\bar{\beta}_{h}\,e^{-2t}dt=\int\eta_{h}\overline{L_{h}^{\star}(\beta_{h})}\,e^{-2t}dt=0\,,

which is not possible.

Proof of Lemma
A.4.2
.

We begin by proving the following claim:

(Cl)

For any
z\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
, with
m>1
, such that

m^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m}(\alpha_{z})-z\alpha_{z}=0
,
there exists
\beta_{z}\in\mathcal{H}^{e}
such that

m^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\star}(\beta_{z})-\bar{z}\beta_{z}=0\,,

(A.7)

and

\left\langle\alpha_{z},\beta_{z}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{e}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{A(t)}{(\Xi(t)-z)^{2}}\varphi_{z}(t)^{2}\,dt\,,

(A.8)

where
\varphi_{z}
is the unique solution in
\mathcal{H}^{e}
of
\varphi_{z}^{\prime\prime}-m^{2}\varphi_{z}=\alpha_{z}
.

To that aim we first observe that the adjoint of
\mathcal{L}_{m}
in
\mathcal{H}^{e}
is given by

\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\star}(\alpha)=m(\Xi\alpha+e^{2t}\mathcal{K}_{m}(A\alpha e^{-2t}))\,,

(A.9)

where
\mathcal{K}_{m}
is the inverse of
-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+m^{2}
as a closed unbounded self-adjoint operator in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Notice that
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
is well defined because
e^{-t}\alpha\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
A\sim e^{2t}
as
t\to-\infty
.

We now observe that, if
z\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
,
m^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m}(\alpha_{z})=z\alpha_{z}
, and
\beta_{z}
is defined by

\beta_{z}:=e^{2t}\frac{\bar{\varphi}_{z}}{\Xi-\bar{z}}=-e^{2t}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{m}(\bar{\alpha}_{z})}{\Xi-\bar{z}}\,,

then

m^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\star}(\beta_{z})=\bar{z}\beta_{z}\,.

(A.10)

Notice that
\beta_{z}\in W^{2,2}_{\textrm{loc}}\cap\mathcal{H}^{e}
decays exponentially fast at
\infty
thanks to the bound
|\varphi_{z}(t)|\leq Ce^{-m|t|}
, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, proven in Lemma
4.5.3
.
Let us now verify (
A.10
):
We first observe that

\alpha_{z}=\frac{A\varphi_{z}}{\Xi-z}\,,

(A.11)

hence

\displaystyle m^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\star}(\beta_{z})

\displaystyle=\Xi\beta_{z}+e^{2t}\mathcal{K}_{m}\left(\frac{A\bar{\varphi}_{z}}{\Xi-\bar{z}}\right)=\Xi\beta_{z}+e^{2t}\mathcal{K}_{m}(\bar{\alpha}_{z})

\displaystyle=\Xi\beta_{z}-(\Xi-\bar{z})\beta_{z}=\bar{z}\beta_{z}\,.

It is now immediate to conclude (
A.8
).

In order to simplify our notation we use
\mathcal{L}_{h}
,
z_{h}
and
m_{h}
in place of
\mathcal{L}_{m_{a}-h}
,
z_{a-h}
and
m_{a}-h
.
Given
\alpha_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{e}
as in the statement of the Lemma we denote by
\varphi_{h}
the unique
L^{2}
solution of
\varphi_{h}^{\prime\prime}-m_{h}^{2}\varphi_{h}=\alpha_{h}
. We now can apply (Cl) above to find
\beta_{h}\in\mathcal{H}^{e}
which solves
m_{h}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{h}^{\star}(\beta_{h})=\bar{z}_{h}\beta_{h}
and such that

\left\langle\alpha_{h},\beta_{h}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{e}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{A(t)}{(\Xi(t)-z_{h})^{2}}\varphi_{h}(t)^{2}\,dt\,.

(A.12)

To conclude the proof it suffices to show that, after appropriately normalizing the functions
\alpha_{h}
(i.e. after multiplying them by an appropriate constant factor, which might depend on
h
) we have

\lim_{h\to 0}\left\langle\alpha_{h},\beta_{h}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{e}}=c\neq 0\,.

(A.13)

Note that for the latter conclusion, which we will prove in the next two steps, we will use the assumption that
\alpha_{h}\neq 0
.

Step 1:
We show that, up to multiplication of
\alpha_{h}
by a suitable constant factor (which might vary with
h
),
\varphi_{h}\to\varphi
in
W^{1,2}
and in
C^{1,\alpha}
, as
h\to 0
, where
\varphi\in W^{2,\infty}
is a nontrivial solution to

-\frac{d^{2}\varphi}{dt^{2}}+m_{a}^{2}\varphi+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}\varphi=0\,.

(A.14)

By Remark
4.6.1
, the nontriviality of
\varphi
implies
\varphi(a)\neq 0
, and hence, up to multiplication by another constant factor, we will assume, without loss of generality, that
\varphi(a)=1
.

Recall that
\varphi_{h}
solves the equation

-\frac{d^{2}\varphi_{h}}{dt^{2}}+m_{h}^{2}\varphi_{h}+\frac{A}{\Xi-z_{h}}\varphi_{h}=0\,.

(A.15)

For the moment, let us normalize the functions so that

\int(|\varphi_{h}^{\prime}|^{2}+m_{h}^{2}|\varphi_{h}|^{2})=1\,,

(A.16)

as in (
4.68
). We then can argue as for the bounds (
4.69
) and (
4.70
) to derive the existence of constants
C
and
\beta>2
(independent of
h
) such that

\left|\varphi_{h}(t)\right|\leq Ce^{-\beta|t|}\quad\forall t\,.

(A.17)

Recalling Section
4.8
, we know that
z_{h}=\Xi(a)+c(a)h+o(h)
, where
c(a)
is a complex number with positive imaginary part, which we denote by
d(a)
. Using the monotonicity of
\Xi
we can write
z_{h}=\Xi(t(h))+i(d(a)h+o(h))
for some
t(h)
which satisfies the bound
|t(h)-a|\leq Ch
for some positive constant
C
. In particular, using the mean value theorem and the fact that the derivative of
\Xi
does not vanish on
[a-1,a+1]
, we get

|\Xi(t)-z_{h}|^{2}\geq C^{-1}(|t-t(h)|^{2}+|h|^{2})\qquad\forall t\in[a-1,a+1]\,,

where
C
is some positive constant. Next, using that
|t(h)-a|\leq Ch
, we conclude the estimate

|\Xi(t)-z_{h}|\geq C^{-1}|t-a|\qquad\forall t\in[a-1,a+1]\,,

with a constant
C
independent of
h
. Since
a
is a zero of
A
, we finally conclude that the functions

\frac{A(t)}{\Xi(t)-z_{h}}

are in fact uniformly bounded, independently of
h
. Using the latter estimate and (
A.17
) we thus infer that

|\varphi_{h}^{\prime\prime}(t)|\leq Ce^{-\beta|t|}\,.

(A.18)

In particular, upon extraction of a subsequence, we can assume that the
\varphi_{h}
converge to a function
\varphi
strongly in
W^{1,2}
, weakly in
W^{2,\infty}
, and hence strongly in
C^{1,\alpha}
for every
\alpha<1
. In particular, because of the normalization (
A.16
),
\varphi
is a nontrivial
W^{2,\infty}
function, satisfying the same exponential decay as in (
A.17
) and (
A.18
). Moreover, given the bound on the functions
\frac{A(t)}{\Xi(t)-z_{h}}
,
\varphi
is in fact a solution of

-\frac{d^{2}\varphi}{dt^{2}}+m_{a}^{2}\varphi+\frac{A}{\Xi-\Xi(a)}\varphi=0\,.

(A.19)

Recalling Remark
4.6.1
,
\varphi(a)\neq 0
and
\varphi
is unique up to a constant factor. In particular, we must have

\liminf_{h\downarrow 0}|\varphi_{h}(a)|>0,

(A.20)

otherwise for a suitable subsequence we would have convergence to a nontrivial solution
\varphi
for which
\varphi(a)=0
. Because of (
A.20
) we can use the different normalization
\varphi_{h}(a)=1
, which in turn implies that
\varphi_{h}
converges (without extracting subsequences) to the unique
W^{2,2}
solution
\varphi
of (
A.19
) which satisfies
\varphi(a)=1
.

Step 2:
We prove that

\lim_{h\to 0}\,\textrm{Im}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{A(t)}{(\Xi(t)-z_{h})^{2}}\varphi_{h}(t)^{2}\,dt=\frac{2A^{\prime}(a)\varphi(a)^{2}}{d(a)\Xi^{\prime}(a)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{s^{2}}{(1+s^{2})^{2}}ds\,.

(A.21)

Recalling that
z_{h}=\Xi(t(h))+i(d(a)h+o(h))
, we write

\displaystyle\quad\;\textrm{Im}\,\left[\frac{A}{(\Xi-z_{h})^{2}}\varphi_{h}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\textrm{Im}\,\left[\frac{A((\Xi-\Xi(t(h)))+i(d(a)h+o(h)))^{2}}{((\Xi-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2})^{2}}(\textrm{Re}\,\varphi_{h}+i\textrm{Im}\,\varphi_{h})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2(d(a)h+o(h))A(\Xi-\Xi(t(h)))}{((\Xi-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2})^{2}}(\mathrm{Re}\,\varphi_{h}^{2}-\textrm{Im}\,\varphi_{h}^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2A}{(\Xi-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}}\textrm{Re}\,\varphi_{h}\textrm{Im}\,\varphi_{h}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{4(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}A}{((\Xi-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2})^{2}}\textrm{Re}\,\varphi_{h}\textrm{Im}\,\varphi_{h}

\displaystyle=:I_{h}+II_{h}+III_{h}\,.

To ease notation we set

f_{h}:=\textrm{Re}\,\varphi_{h}^{2}-\textrm{Im}\,\varphi_{h}^{2}\,,\quad g_{h}:=\textrm{Re}\,\varphi_{h}\,\textrm{Im}\varphi_{h}\,,

(A.22)

and observe that
f_{h}\to\varphi^{2}
,
g_{h}\to 0
as
h\to 0
, where the convergence is, in both cases, in the strong topologies of
L^{2}
and of
C^{\alpha}
, for every
\alpha<1
.
We will show below that:

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int I_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{2A^{\prime}(a)\varphi(a)^{2}}{d(a)\Xi^{\prime}(a)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{s^{2}}{(1+s^{2})^{2}}ds=:L(a)\,,

(A.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int II_{h}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(A.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int III_{h}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(A.25)

Considering that none of the numbers
\Xi^{\prime}(a)
,
A^{\prime}(a)
,
\varphi(a)
, and
d(a)
vanish,
L(a)\neq 0
. This implies (
A.13
) and concludes the proof. We next study separately the three limits above.

Proof of (
A.23
).

There exists
\delta>0
and
r>0
such that for any
h
sufficiently small one has
|\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h))|>\delta
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(a-r/2,a+r/2)
. This implies that

\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(t(h)-r,t(h)+r)}I_{h}=0\,,

(A.26)

hence, we are left with

\lim_{h\to 0}2hd(a)\int_{t(h)-r}^{t(h)+r}\frac{A(t)(\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h))}{((\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2})^{2}}f_{h}(t)\,dt\,.

(A.27)

We change variables according to
t=t(h)+sh
:

\displaystyle C(h)

\displaystyle:=2\int_{-\frac{r}{h}}^{\frac{r}{h}}s\left(\frac{A(t(h)+sh)}{h}\frac{\Xi(t(h)+sh)-\Xi(t(h))}{sh}\right)\times

\displaystyle\left(s^{2}\left(\frac{\Xi(t(h)+sh)-\Xi(t(h))}{sh}\right)^{2}+(d(a)+o(h)/h)^{2}\right)^{-2}f_{h}(t(h)+sh)\,ds\,.

Notice that, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\Xi(t(h)+sh)-\Xi(t(h))}{sh}=\Xi^{\prime}(a)\,.

(A.28)

Moreover the monotonicity of
\Xi
implies that

1/C\leq\left|\frac{\Xi(t(h)+sh)-\Xi(t(h))}{sh}\right|\leq C\quad\text{for any $s\in(-r/h,r/h)$}\,.

(A.29)

Notice that

\frac{A(t(h)+sh)}{h}=\frac{A(t(h)+sh)-A(t(h))}{h}+\frac{A(t(h))-A(a)}{h}\,,

(A.30)

hence, up to extracting a subsequence
h_{i}\to 0
, we have

\frac{A(t(h_{i})+sh_{i})}{h_{i}}\to A^{\prime}(a)s+x

(A.31)

for some
x\in\mathbb{R}
(recall that
|t(h)-a|\leq Ch
and note that
x
might depend on the subsequence).

Collecting all the estimates above, and using the dominated convergence theorem we deduce that, along the subsequence
h_{i}
,

\lim_{i\to\infty}C(h_{i})=2\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{s(A^{\prime}(a)s+x)\Xi^{\prime}(a)}{(s^{2}\Xi^{\prime}(a)^{2}+d(a)^{2})^{2}}\varphi(a)^{2}\,ds=\frac{2A^{\prime}(a)\varphi(a)^{2}}{d(a)\Xi^{\prime}(a)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{s^{2}}{(1+s^{2})^{2}}ds\,.

(A.32)

Observe that the limit does not depend on
x
and hence does not depend on the chosen subsequence.

Proof of (
A.25
).
Arguing as we did for
I_{h}
and using that
g_{h}\to 0
in
C^{1/2}
, as
h\to 0
, we easily deduce that

\lim_{h\to 0}\int III_{h}=0\,.

(A.33)

Proof of (
A.24
).

We need to show that

\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{A(t)}{(\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}}g_{h}(t)\,dt=0\,.

(A.34)

Observe that
G_{h}:=g_{h}/|\varphi_{h}|^{2}=|\varphi_{h}|^{-2}\textrm{Re}\,\varphi_{h}\textrm{Im}\,\varphi_{h}
, and in particular,
|G_{h}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Moreover there exists
r>0
such that
G_{h}\to 0
in
(a-r,a+r)
in the
C^{\alpha}
topology for every
\alpha<1
(here, we are using that
|\varphi_{h}(a)|^{2}\to|\varphi(a)|^{2}\neq 0
as
h\to 0
).
We write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\frac{A(t)|\varphi_{h}(t)|^{2}}{(\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}}G_{h}(t)\,dt

(A.35)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{A(t)|\varphi_{h}(t)|^{2}}{(\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}}(G_{h}(t)-G_{h}(t(h)))dt\,,

(A.36)

where we took advantage of the identity

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{A(t)|\varphi_{h}(t)|^{2}}{(\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}}\,dt=0\,,

(A.37)

proven in (
4.59
).

Arguing as we did for
I_{h}
we can reduce the problem to show

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}

\displaystyle\int_{t(h)-r}^{t(h)+r}\frac{A(t)|\varphi_{h}(t)|^{2}}{(\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}}(G_{h}(t)-G_{h}(t(h)))\,dt=0\,.

(A.38)

We split the integral to the sum of

\displaystyle J_{1}(h)

\displaystyle:=\int_{t(h)-r}^{t(h)+r}\frac{(A(t)-A(t(h))|\varphi_{h}(t)|^{2}}{(\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}}(G_{h}(t)-G_{h}(t(h)))\,dt

\displaystyle J_{2}(h)

\displaystyle:=A(t(h))\int_{t(h)-r}^{t(h)+r}\frac{|\varphi_{h}(t)|^{2}}{(\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h)))^{2}+(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}}(G_{h}(t)-G_{h}(t(h)))\,dt\,.

Next observe that, in the interval that interests us, the following inequalities hold provided
r
and
h
are sufficiently small:

\displaystyle|A(t)-A(t(h))|

\displaystyle\leq C|t-t(h)|

\displaystyle|A(t(h)|

\displaystyle=|A(t(h))-A(a)|\leq C|t(h)-a|\leq Ch

\displaystyle|G_{h}(t)-G_{h}(t(h))|

\displaystyle\leq\|G_{h}\|_{C^{1/2}(a-r,a+r)}|t-t(h)|^{1/2}

\displaystyle|\Xi(t)-\Xi(t(h))|

\displaystyle\geq C^{-1}|t-t(h)|

\displaystyle(d(a)h+o(h))^{2}

\displaystyle\geq C^{-1}h^{2}\,.

Since
\|\varphi_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C
, we can change variable in the integrals to
\sigma=t-t(h)
and estimate them as follows:

\displaystyle|J_{1}(h)|

\displaystyle\leq C\|G_{h}\|_{C^{1/2}(a-r,a+r)}\int_{-r/2}^{r/2}\sigma^{-1/2}\,d\sigma\leq C\|G_{h}\|_{C^{1/2}}r^{1/2}\,,

\displaystyle|J_{2}(h)|

\displaystyle\leq Ch\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\sigma^{1/2}}{\sigma^{2}+C^{-1}h^{2}}\,d\sigma=Ch^{1/2}\int\frac{\tau^{1/2}}{\tau^{2}+C^{-1}}d\tau\leq Ch^{1/2}\,.

Clearly
J_{2}(h)\to 0
, while
J_{1}(h)\to 0
because
\|G_{h}\|_{C^{1/2}(a-r,a+r)}\to 0
.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of technical statements

B.1.
Proof of Remark
1.0.2

More generally, we will show here that, for any
q_{0}\in[1,2[
and
q_{1}\in]2,\infty]
, it is true that

\lVert K_{2}*\omega\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(q_{0},q_{1})(\lVert\omega\rVert_{L^{q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\lVert\omega\rVert_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})

for all
\omega\in L^{q_{0}}\cap L^{q_{1}}
.
Indeed, passing to polar coordinates, one sees that
K_{2}|_{B_{1}}\in L^{q_{1}^{*}}(B_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
K_{2}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{1}}\in L^{q_{0}^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where
q_{0}^{*},q_{1}^{*}
are given by
\frac{1}{q_{i}}+\frac{1}{q_{i}^{*}}=1
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. Hölder’s inequality implies that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\begin{split}\lvert(K_{2}*\omega)(x)\rvert&=\lvert((K_{2}\mathbf{1}_{B_{1}})*\omega)(x)+((K_{2}(1-\mathbf{1}_{B_{1}}))*\omega)(x)\rvert\\
&\leq\lVert K_{2}\rVert_{L^{q_{1}^{*}}(B_{1})}\lVert\omega\rVert_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\lVert K_{2}\rVert_{L^{q_{0}^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{1})}\lVert\omega\rVert_{L^{q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
&\leq C(q_{0},q_{1})(\lVert\omega\rVert_{L^{q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\lVert\omega\rVert_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}).\end{split}

Since
x
is arbitrary, this achieves a proof of the claim above.

B.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.0.3

Existence.
The existence argument is a classical density argument. Take any sequence
(\omega_{0}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions in
L^{1}\cap C^{\infty}_{c}
that converges strongly in
L^{1}
to
\omega_{0}
. Analogously, pick a sequence of smooth functions
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T])
converging in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T])
to
f
and satisfying the bound
\|f_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}
for a.e.
t
. Then, let
\omega^{(n)}
denote the solution of the corresponding Cauchy problem of the Euler equations in vorticity form. The existence of such solutions is a classical well-known fact, see for instance
[
30
, Theorem A]
. Following Remark
1.0.4
, these solutions satisfy all the à priori estimates needed in Proposition
2.2.3
. Therefore, following the proof of Proposition
2.2.3
, one obtains, in the limit
n\to\infty
, a solution
\omega\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})
of the given Cauchy problem. Furthermore, since the à priori estimates of Remark
1.0.4
are uniform in
n
, one gets
K_{2}*\omega\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
.

Remark B.2.1
.

The proof of Proposition
2.2.3
has a fixed force
f
but a straightforward adaptation of the arguments handles the case above, namely with a sequence of forces
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that converges in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T])
to a given
f
. More precisely the only difference occurs in the term
I_{4}(k)
of (
B.6
), which anyway enjoys convergence to the same limit.

Uniqueness.
The uniqueness proof needs two important facts. The first is a well-known ODE inequality, whose short proof is given, for the reader’s convenience, at the end of the section.

Lemma B.2.2
.

Let
T>0
and let
E:[0,T]\to[0,\infty[
be a differentiable function satisfying

\dot{E}(t)\leq pME(t)^{1-1/p}\quad\text{ and }\quad E(0)=0

(B.1)

for some fixed
M>0
. Then
E(t)\leq(Mt)^{p}
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

The second is the classical Calderón-Zygmund
L^{p}
estimate, where we need the sharp
p
-dependence of the corresponding constant. This fact is also well known, cf. for instance
[
29
, Formula (8.45), page 322]
).

Lemma B.2.3
.

For every
p_{0}>1
there is a constant
c(p_{0})
with the following property.
If
v=K_{2}*\omega
for some
\omega\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
p\in[p_{0},\infty[
, then
\lVert Dv\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq pc\lVert\omega\rVert_{L^{p}}
.

Now, let
v_{1}=K_{2}*\omega_{1},v_{2}=K_{2}*\omega_{2}
be two solutions of (
1.1
) satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
1.0.3
and note that
w:=v_{1}-v_{2}
solves

\partial_{t}w+(v_{1}\cdot\nabla)w+(w\cdot\nabla)v_{2}=-\nabla(p_{1}-p_{2})

(B.2)

(where
p_{1},p_{2}
are the pressures corresponding to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
). Clearly

E(t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|w(x,t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|v_{1}(x,t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|v_{2}(x,t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty

is a bounded function on
[0,T]
.

We scalar multiply (
B.2
) with
w
, integrate by parts and use the divergence free conditions of
v_{1},v_{2}
, and
w
to conclude and

\displaystyle\dot{E}(t)=

\displaystyle-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}((w\cdot\nabla)v_{2})w\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|w(x,t)|^{2}\lvert Dv_{2}(x,t)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\lVert\nabla v_{2}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{p}}\lVert w(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{2/p}\lVert w(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2-2/p}\,.

Using Remark
1.0.2
, we also have

\begin{split}&\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert w(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\lVert v_{1}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\lVert v_{2}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}})\\
&\hskip 19.91684pt\leq C\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\lVert\omega_{1}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{1}}+\lVert\omega_{1}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\lVert\omega_{2}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{1}}+\lVert\omega_{2}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}})<\infty\,.\end{split}

Next fix any
p\geq 2
. From Lemma
B.2.3
and the classical
L^{p}
interpolation we conclude

\lVert Dv_{2}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq pc\lVert\omega_{2}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq pc\lVert\omega_{2}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{1})}^{1/p}\lVert\omega_{2}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty})}^{1-1/p}.

Therefore,
\dot{E}(t)\leq pM_{p}E(t)^{1-1/p}

with

\displaystyle M_{p}

\displaystyle=2\lVert w\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty})}^{2/p}c\lVert\omega_{2}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{1})}^{1/p}\lVert\omega_{2}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty})}^{1-1/p}

\displaystyle\leq 2c\left({\textstyle{\frac{1}{p}}}\lVert w\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty})}^{2}\lVert\omega_{2}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{1})}+\left(1-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{p}}}\right)\lVert\omega_{2}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2c(\lVert w\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty})}^{2}\lVert\omega_{2}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{1})}+\lVert\omega_{2}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty})})=:M<\infty\,.

We can thus apply
Lemma
B.2.2
to obtain that
E(t)\leq(M_{p}t)^{p}\leq(Mt)^{p}
.
In particular, for any
t\leq\frac{1}{2M}
we have
E(t)\leq\frac{1}{2^{p}}
and we can let
p
tend to
\infty
to infer
E(t)=0
. Since the same estimates apply to any translation
\tilde{E}(t):=E(t+t_{0})
of the function
E
, we immediately conclude that
E
vanishes identically, namely that
v_{1}=v_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]
.

Proof of Lemma
B.2.2
.

Fix an arbitrary
t_{0}\leq T
and note that if
E(t_{0})=0
there is nothing to show. Hence assume
E(t_{0})>0
and set
a:=\sup\{t:E(t)=0\text{ and }t\leq t_{0}\}
(note that the set is nonempty because
E(0)=0
).
E(a)=0
by continuity of
E
and clearly
E(t)>0
for all
t\in]a,t_{0}]
. Therefore, we can divide (
B.1
) by
E(t)^{1-1/p}
to obtain that
\dot{E}(t)E^{1/p-1}(t)\leq pM

for all
t\in]a,t_{0}]
. Integrating both sides gives

\int_{a}^{t_{0}}\dot{E}(t)E^{1/p-1}(t)\leq pM(t_{0}-a)\,.

(B.3)

But the left hand side equals
pE^{1/p}(t_{0})-pE^{1/p}(a)=pE^{1/p}(t_{0})
, from which we infer

E^{1/p}(t_{0})\leq M(t_{0}-a)\leq Mt_{0}
.
∎

B.3.
Proof of Proposition
2.2.3

Recall first the following classical metrizability result of weak
∗
topologies of separable Banach spaces.

Lemma B.3.1

(Metrizability Lemma)
.

Let
X
be a separable Banach space and let
K\subset X^{*}
be weakly
∗
-compact. Then
K
is metrizable in the weak
∗
topology inherited from
X^{*}
and a metric that induces this topology is given by

d(l,\tilde{l})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{-n}\min\{1,|l(x_{n})-\tilde{l}(x_{n})|\},

(B.4)

where
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is any sequence in
X
such that
\{x_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is dense in
X
.

Now on to the proof of Proposition
2.2.3
. We will prove convergence of the
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}
to a
\omega_{\varepsilon}
for fixed
\varepsilon
and
k\to\infty
in the space
C([0,T];K)
, where
K:=\{u\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}}\leq R\}
is equipped with the weak
∗
topology inherited from
L^{q}_{\text{w}}
. (We will talk about the choice of
q
later.) Here,
R
is the uniform bound obtained in (
2.24
) of Corollary
2.2.2
. Note that since every
L^{q}
space is reflexive, one can work just as well with the weak topology on
K
. Let
(\phi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of smooth functions such that
\{\phi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is dense in every
L^{q}
. The metric given by (
B.4
) now induces the topology of
K
, and it does not depend on
q
. Therefore, using the uniform bound (
2.24
), we conclude that
the choice of
q
does not matter
. It is sufficient to prove the statement of Proposition
2.2.3
for one fixed
q\in]1,p]
in order to prove it for all
q\in]1,p]
.

Claim B.3.2
.

\omega_{\varepsilon,k}
, seen as functions from
[0,T]
to
K
, are equicontinuous (for simplicity we define each
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)
on the interval
[0,t_{k}]
as constantly equal to
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t_{k})
).

Proof.

For
\tilde{\omega},\hat{\omega}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, let

d_{i}(\tilde{\omega},\hat{\omega})\overset{\text{Def.}}{=}\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\tilde{\omega}-\hat{\omega})\phi_{i}\mathrm{d}x\right\rvert.

Since each
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}
solves the Euler equations in vorticity form, we can estimate

\displaystyle\;\;d_{i}(\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(t,\cdot),\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(s,\cdot))

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{\tau}\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\sigma,x)\phi_{i}(x)\,d\sigma\,dx\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{s}^{t}((K_{2}*\omega_{\varepsilon,k})\cdot\nabla)\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(x,\sigma)+f(x,\sigma)\phi_{i}(x)\,d\sigma\,dx\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\nabla\phi_{i}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{s}^{t}\lvert K_{2}*\omega_{\varepsilon,k}\rvert\lvert\omega_{\varepsilon,k}\rvert\,d\sigma\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad+\lVert\phi_{i}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{s}^{t}|f(x,\sigma)|\,d\sigma\,dx

\displaystyle\leq C(\lVert\nabla\phi_{i}\rVert_{\infty}+\|\phi_{i}\|_{\infty})\lvert s-t\rvert^{\gamma}

(B.5)

whenever
t\geq s\geq t_{k}
, where
\gamma=\gamma(\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}})
. Indeed, the last inequality follows from
f\in L^{r}_{t}L^{1}_{x}
for some
r=r(\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}})>1
(see the proof of Lemma
2.1.2
). Let
\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
. We can find a
N\in\mathbb{N}
(depending on
\tilde{\varepsilon}
) such that
\sum_{n=N^{+}1}^{\infty}2^{-n}\leq\frac{\tilde{\varepsilon}}{2}
. If

\lvert t-s\rvert\leq\left(\frac{\tilde{\varepsilon}}{2NC\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}(\lVert\nabla\phi_{i}\rVert_{\infty}+\|\phi_{i}\|_{\infty})}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}},

where
C
is the constant from (
B.5
), then, by the bound in (
B.5
), we get

d(\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(t,\cdot),\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(s,\cdot))\leq\frac{\tilde{\varepsilon}}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\tilde{\varepsilon}}{2N}=\tilde{\varepsilon}.\qed

By the Banach-Alaoglu theorem, bounded subsets are relatively compact in the weak
∗
topology. Therefore, using reflexivity, for every
t\in[0,T]
, the bounded set
\{\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t):k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is also relatively compact in
L^{q}_{\text{w}}
.

Therefore, using Arzelà-Ascoli, we can conclude that there exists a subsequence of
(\omega_{\varepsilon,k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, not relabeled, that converges in
C([0,T];L_{\text{w}}^{q})
, for every
q
, to the same
\omega_{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T];L_{\text{w}}^{q})
.

Claim B.3.3
.

The
\omega_{\varepsilon}
is a solution of the Euler equations in vorticity formulation.

Proof.

We have, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\phi\in C_{\text{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T])
with
\phi(\cdot,T)=0
, (cf. (
1.3
))

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(x,t_{k})\phi(x,t_{k})\,\mathrm{d}x}_{=:I_{1}(k)}+\underbrace{\int_{t_{k}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ \omega_{\varepsilon,k}(x,t)\partial_{t}\phi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=:I_{2}(k)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{t_{k}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(x,t)((K_{2}*_{x}\omega_{\varepsilon,k})(x,t)\cdot\nabla)\phi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=:I_{3}(k)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{t_{k}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x,t)\phi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=:I_{4}(k)}=0\,.

(B.6)

The term
I_{4}(k)
converges to

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}f(x,t)\phi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

By the convergence of the
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}I_{2}(k)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\partial_{t}\phi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

By the definition of the initial condition of
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}
(cf. (
2.20
)),
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t_{k})
converges strongly in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
\tilde{\omega}(\cdot,0)=\omega_{0}=\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,0)
. Therefore,

\lim_{k\to\infty}I_{1}(k)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,0)\phi(x,0)\,\mathrm{d}x.

It therefore only remains to prove the convergence of
I_{3}
, for which we will require yet another claim.

Claim B.3.4
.

For every
r\in[2,\infty[
and every
t\in[0,T]
, the set
\{v_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t):k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is compact in
L^{r}(B_{R})
for every
R>0
.

Proof.

From (
2.24
), we know that
\|v_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C
for some constant
C
that is independent of
t
. Recall that
v_{\varepsilon,k}=\nabla^{\bot}\psi_{\varepsilon,k}
, where
\psi_{\varepsilon,k}
solves
\Delta\psi_{\varepsilon,k}=\omega_{\varepsilon,k}
. Therefore, using the Calderón-Zygmund inequality, one gets

\lVert\nabla v_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\lVert\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Since the
L^{2}
norms of the
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)
are uniformly bounded, we can conclude that

\sup_{k\in\infty}\lVert\nabla v_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{2}}<\infty.

Hence we conclude the compactness in
L^{r}(B_{R})
from Rellich’s Theorem.
∎

Therefore, the
v_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)
converge to
v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
strongly in every
L^{r}(B_{R})
with
r\in[2,\infty[
. Moreover, thanks to (
2.24
), we can apply the dominated convergence theorem by Lebesgue to conclude that
v_{\varepsilon,k}\to v_{\varepsilon}
as
k\to\infty
in the space
L^{1}([0,T];L^{r}(B_{R}))
for every
r\in[2,\infty[
.

By definition,

\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(v_{\varepsilon,k}\cdot\nabla)\phi-\omega_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\phi=\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(v_{\varepsilon,k}-v_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla\phi+(\omega_{\varepsilon,k}-\omega_{\varepsilon})v_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\phi\,.

We thus rewrite

\displaystyle I_{3}(k)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{R}}\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(v_{\varepsilon,k}-v_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}\underbrace{\int_{B_{R}}(\omega_{\varepsilon,k}-\omega_{\varepsilon})v_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx}_{=:J_{k}(t)}\,dt\,.

(B.7)

Observe first that, for each fixed
t
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}J_{k}(t)=0\,,

since
\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)-\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
converges weakly to
0
in
L^{2}
, while
v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla\phi(\cdot,t)
is a fixed
L^{2}
function. On the other hand

|J_{k}(t)|\leq\|\nabla\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}(\|\omega_{\varepsilon,k}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}})\|v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Therefore the second integral in (
B.7
) converges to
0
. The first integral can be bounded by

\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v_{\varepsilon,k}-v_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}([0,T],L^{2}(B_{R}))}\|\omega_{\varepsilon,k}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{2}(B_{R}))}

and converges to
0
as well.
∎

B.4.
Proof of Lemma
2.4.1

Consider
\vartheta\in L^{2}_{m}\cap\mathscr{S}
for
m\geq 2
and let
v:=K_{2}*\vartheta
. We first claim that

\int_{B_{R}}v=0\qquad\qquad\mbox{for every $R>0$.}

(B.8)

With (
B.8
) at our disposal, since
\|Dv\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|\vartheta\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, we use the Poincaré inequality to conclude

R^{-1}\|v\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}+\|Dv\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\leq C\|\vartheta\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

(B.9)

for a geometric constant
C
. This is then enough to infer the remaining conclusions of the lemma.

In order to achieve (
B.8
) observe first that
v=\nabla^{\perp}h
, where
h
is the unique potential-theoretic solution of
\Delta h=\vartheta
, given by
h=K*\vartheta
with
K(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|
. Since
K(R_{\theta}x)=K(x)
and
\vartheta(x)=\vartheta(R_{2\pi/m}x)
, it follows that
h(R_{2\pi/m}x)=h(x)
, i.e.
h
is
m
-fold symmetric. Therefore
R_{-2\pi/m}\nabla h(R_{2\pi/m}x)=\nabla h(x)
. In particular, integrating in
x
and using that the rotation is a measure-preserving transformation of the disk, we conclude

\int_{B_{R}}\nabla h=R_{2\pi/m}\int_{B_{R}}\nabla h\,,

and thus,

\int_{B_{R}}\nabla h=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}R_{2k\pi/m}\int_{B_{R}}\nabla h\,.

However, since
m\geq 2
,
\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}R_{2k\pi/m}=0
, showing that
\int_{B_{R}}\nabla h=0
.

Remark B.4.1
.

We next show that it is not possible to find a continuous extension of the operator
L^{2}\cap\mathscr{S}\ni\vartheta\mapsto K_{2}*\vartheta\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
to the whole
L^{2}
. First of all we observe that, if such an extension exists, it then needs to coincide with
K_{2}*\vartheta
when
\vartheta\in L^{1}\cap L^{2}
. We next exhibit a sequence of divergence free vector fields
\{v_{k}\}\subset W^{1,1}\cap W^{1,2}
with the property that
\omega_{k}=\curl v_{k}
converge to
0
strongly in
L^{2}
but
v_{k}
converge locally to a constant vector field
v_{0}\neq 0
. In order to do this, we first define the following functions
\phi_{k}
on the positive real axis:

\phi_{k}(r):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1+\frac{3}{4\ln k}&\mbox{for $r\leq\frac{k}{2}$}\\
\\
1+\frac{3}{4\ln k}-\frac{1}{k^{2}\ln k}\left(r-\frac{k}{2}\right)^{2}&\mbox{for $\frac{k}{2}\leq r\leq k$}\\
\\
1+\frac{1}{2\ln k}-\frac{1}{\ln k}\ln\frac{r}{k}&\mbox{for $k\leq r\leq k^{2}$}\\
\\
\frac{1}{2k^{4}\ln k}(r-2k^{2})^{2}&\mbox{for $k^{2}\leq r\leq 2k^{2}$}\\
\\
0&\mbox{for $r\geq 2k^{2}$}\,.\end{array}\right.

Observe that
\phi_{k}
is
C^{1}
and its derivative is Lipschitz. Next we define the stream functions

\psi_{k}(x)=-\phi_{k}(|x|)v_{0}^{\perp}\cdot x

and the vector field
v_{k}(x)=\nabla^{\perp}\psi_{k}(x)
. By construction
v_{k}
is divergence free, compactly supported, and Lipschitz. In particular, it belongs to
W^{1,p}
for every
p
. Moreover,
v_{k}
equals
(1+\frac{3}{4\ln k})v_{0}
on
B_{k/2}
and it thus follows that, as
k\to\infty
,
v_{k}
converges locally to the constant vector field
v_{0}
. It remains to check that
\curl v_{k}=\Delta\psi_{k}
converges to
0
strongly in
L^{2}
. We compute

\Delta\psi_{k}=-\underbrace{v_{0}^{\perp}\cdot x\,\Delta(\phi_{k}(|x|))}_{=:f_{k}}-\underbrace{\nabla(\phi_{k}(|x|))\cdot v_{0}^{\perp}}_{=:g_{k}}\,

and we seek to bound
f_{k}
and
g_{k}
pointwise. For what concerns
f_{k}
observe that
\Delta\phi_{k}
vanishes on
|x|\leq\frac{k}{2}
,
k\leq|x|\leq k^{2}
, and
2k^{2}\leq|x|
. On the remaining regions, using the formula for the Laplacian in polar coordinates, we can estimate

|f_{k}(x)|\leq|v_{0}||x|(|\phi^{\prime\prime}(|x|)|+|x|^{-1}|\phi^{\prime}(|x|)|)\,.

In particular, we conclude

|f_{k}(|x|)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|\ln k}\,,

for a constant
C
independent of
k
.
As for
g_{k}
, it vanishes for
|x|\leq\frac{k}{2}
and
|x|\geq k^{2}
, and where it does not vanish we have the estimate

|g_{k}(x)|\leq|v_{0}||\psi^{\prime}(|x|)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|\ln k}\,,

again for a constant
C
independent of
k
. Passing to polar coordinates, we can thus estimate

\displaystyle\|\Delta\psi_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{(\ln k)^{2}}\int_{k/2}^{2k^{2}}\frac{1}{r}\,dr=\frac{C}{(\ln k)^{2}}\left(\ln(2k^{2})-\ln{\textstyle{\frac{k}{2}}}\right)=\frac{C\ln(4k)}{(\ln k)^{2}}\,.
